He arrived at the hotel in a state of excitement involving an almost abnormal sensitiveness to impressions; but that was soon drowned deep in dreamless slumbers of healthy exhaustion; and when he came to look back through a haze of days, of which each had made its separate and imperative demand upon his budding emotions, he found his store of memories strangely dulled and disarticulate.

The earliest definite picture was that of himself, a small but vastly important figure, nursing a heavy heart in a dark corner of a fiacre. Beside him sat a man who swore fretfully into his moustache whenever the whimpering of the boy threatened to develop into honest bawls: a strange creature, with pockets full of candy and a way with little boys in public surly and domineering, in private timid and propitiatory. It was raining monotonously, with that melancholy persistence which is the genius of Parisian winters; and the paving of the interminable strange streets was as black glass shot with coloured lights. Some of the streets roared like famished beasts, others again were silent, if with a silence no less sinister. The rain made incessant crepitation on the roof of the fiacre, and the windows wept without respite. Within the cab a smell of mustiness contended feebly with the sickening reek of a cigar which the man was forever relighting and which as often turned cold between his teeth. Outside, unwearying hoofs were beating their deadly rhythm, *cloppetty-clop*.﻿ ﻿…

Back of all this lurked something formlessly alluring, something sad and sweet and momentous, which belonged very personally to the child but which he could never realize. Memory crept blindly toward it over a sword-wide bridge that had no end. There had been (or the boy had dreamed it) a long, weariful journey by railroad, the sequel to one by boat more brief but wholly loathsome. Beyond this point memory failed though sick with yearning. And the child gave over his instinctive but rather inconsecutive efforts to retrace his history: his daily life at Troyon’s furnished compelling and obliterating interests.

Madame saw to that.

===Chinese===
凭着这样的设备，特鲁瓦旅馆的客人们倒心满意足。一来人数本就不多，二来几乎全是中年的布尔乔亚——一个天生憎恶革新的阶层。他们对特鲁瓦旅馆照单全收：房间称心极了，房价也公道。何必多事，去搅扰如此一家谨慎而私密的老店的万世清平？在那儿，人人大可随心所欲，只要账单付得尚算按时，并且把深更半夜伺候铃索的那只手打点得活络滋润。特鲁瓦老板出身店家部族，心胸宽和；至于老板娘太太，她除了金币之外一概不放在心上。……

1893 年冬天一个湿漉漉的夜里，那孩子来到了特鲁瓦旅馆——此人长大成人后，自称迈克尔·兰亚德。

他当年约莫四五岁：正是那样的年纪，意识方才开始认出自己的独立性，记忆的登记也随心所欲、全无章法。抵达旅馆时他正处在一种亢奋状态里，对种种印象的感受力近乎反常地敏锐；但这点敏锐很快便溺毙于健康疲惫带来的无梦酣睡深处；待他日后隔着一片朦胧的日子回望——每一天都曾对他的萌芽情感提出各自十万火急的索求——他发觉自己的记忆库存古怪地黯淡而脱了榫。

最早的清晰画面是这样：他自己，一个小小却自以为顶顶要紧的身影，缩在一辆出租马车（fiacre）的暗角里，抱着一颗沉重的心。他身旁坐着个男人，每当孩子的呜咽眼看要发展为货真价实的嚎啕，那人便冲着自己的小胡子烦躁地咒骂：一个古怪的家伙，口袋里塞满糖果，对付小男孩的手法，人前凶横专断，人后怯懦讨好。雨下得单调，带着那种忧郁的不屈不挠——此乃巴黎冬天的天才；漫无尽头、陌生透顶的街道，路面像黑玻璃，游走着斑斓的灯光。有的街道吼声如饿疯的野兽，有的街道则一片沉默——虽说那沉默之不祥也不遑多让。雨点在车顶上噼啪不歇，车窗泪流不止。车厢里，一股霉味有气无力地同雪茄令人作呕的臭气搏斗；那男人不停地将它重新点燃，它又总在他齿间冷掉。车外，不知疲倦的马蹄敲着那致命的节拍，*嘚嘚——嘚嘚*。……

这一切背后蛰伏着某种无形而诱人的东西，某种又悲伤又甘甜、又干系重大的东西，它极其私人地属于这孩子，他却永远无法把握。记忆朝着它盲目爬行，走在一座剑一样窄、望不到头的桥上。曾经有过（或者是那孩子梦见的）一段冗长疲沓的火车旅程，前接一段更为短促却十足可憎的船程。过了这一点，记忆便告阙如，尽管它渴慕得发呕。于是孩子放弃了那种出于本能却颇不连贯的、追溯自己来历的努力：他在特鲁瓦旅馆的日常营生，供给了他既勾魂夺魄、又足以抹煞一切的种种关切。

这件事，自有老板娘督办。

===Original===
It was Madame who took charge of him when the strange man dragged him crying from the cab, through a cold, damp place gloomy with shadows, and upstairs to a warm bright bedroom: a formidable body, this Madame, with cold eyes and many hairy moles, who made odd noises in her throat while she undressed the little boy with the man standing by, noises meant to sound compassionate and maternal but, to the child at least, hopelessly otherwise.

Then drowsiness stealing upon one over a pillow wet with tears﻿ ﻿… oblivion.﻿ ﻿…

And Madame it was who ruled with iron hand the strange new world to which the boy awakened.

The man was gone by morning, and the child never saw him again; but inasmuch as those about him understood no English and he no French, it was some time before he could grasp the false assurances of Madame that his father had gone on a journey but would presently return. The child knew positively that the man was not his father, but when he was able to make this correction the matter had faded into insignificance: life had become too painful to leave time or inclination for the adjustment of such minor and incidental questions as one’s parentage.

The little boy soon learned to know himself as Marcel, which wasn’t his name, and before long was unaware he had ever had another. As he grew older he passed as Marcel Troyon; but by then he had forgotten how to speak English.

A few days after his arrival the warm, bright bedchamber was exchanged for a cold dark closet opening off Madame’s boudoir, a cupboard furnished with a rickety cot and a broken chair, lacking any provision for heat or light, and ventilated solely by a transom over the door; and inasmuch as Madame shared the French horror of draughts and so kept her boudoir hermetically sealed nine months of the year, the transom didn’t mend matters much. But that closet formed the boy’s sole refuge, if a precarious one, through several years; there alone was he ever safe from kicks and cuffs and scoldings for faults beyond his comprehension; but he was never permitted a candle, and the darkness and loneliness made the place one of haunted terror to the sensitive and imaginative nature of a growing child.

===Chinese===
当那陌生的男人哭着喊着把他从马车上拖下来，穿过一处又冷又潮、阴影沉沉的地方，上楼送进一间温暖明亮的卧室时，接手照管他的正是老板娘。这位老板娘，好一尊威严的躯壳：眼睛冰冷，多毛的痣星星点点；她替小男孩脱衣裳、那男人侍立一旁时，喉咙里发出种种怪声——那声音本想显得慈悲而富于母性，但至少在孩子听来，恰恰是无可救药的相反。

随后，睡意悄悄袭来，漫过泪水濡湿的枕头……遗忘。……

而以铁腕君临这孩子醒来所见的陌生新世界的，也正是这位老板娘。

翌日清晨，那男人已经不在了，孩子也再没见过他；只是他周围的人不懂英语，他又不懂法语，隔了好一阵子他才弄懂老板娘那套假保证——说他父亲出门远行，不久便回。孩子确切地知道那男人并非他父亲；可等到他有能力提出这项更正时，事情早已淡薄得无关紧要：生活已苦得叫人既腾不出工夫、也提不起兴致，去厘清诸如自己身世这般细枝末节的小问题了。

小男孩很快便学会应「马塞尔」这个名字——这本不是他的名字——没多久，他已浑然不觉自己曾有过另一个。年岁渐长，他便顶着马塞尔·特鲁瓦（Marcel Troyon）的名头行走；只是到那时，他已忘了怎么说英语。

来后没几天，那间温暖明亮的卧房便换成一间又冷又黑的小间，门开在老板娘的闺房侧翼——一个柜橱似的斗室，陈设一张摇摇欲坠的小床和一把破椅，取暖照明一概欠奉，通风全凭门楣上一扇气窗；而老板娘与全体法国人同仇敌忾地痛恨穿堂风，一年倒有九个月把闺房封得密不透风，那扇气窗也就于事无补甚微。但那斗室毕竟是孩子好几年间唯一的避难所——尽管凶多吉少；唯独在那里，他才能幸免于为他全然莫解的过错而挨的踢、揍与呵斥；但他从不获准点一支蜡烛，黑暗与孤寂使那地方，对一颗日渐长大的、敏感而耽于幻想的童心而言，成了鬼魅横行的恐怖之乡。

===Original===
He was, however, never insufficiently fed; and the luxury of forgetting misery in sleep could not well be denied him.

By day, until of age to go to school, he played apprehensively in the hallways with makeshift toys, a miserable, dejected little body with his heart in his mouth at every sudden footfall, very much in the way of *femmes-de-chambre* who had nothing in common with the warmhearted, impulsive, pitiful serving women of fiction. They complained of him to Madame, and Madame came promptly to cuff him. He soon learned an almost uncanny cunning in the art of effacing himself, when she was imminent, to be as still as death and to move with the silence of a wraith. Not infrequently his huddled immobility in a shadowy corner escaped her notice as she passed. But it always exasperated her beyond measure to look up, when she fancied herself alone, and become aware of the wide-eyed, terrified stare of the transfixed boy.﻿ ﻿…

That he was privileged to attend school at all was wholly due to a great fear that obsessed Madame of doing anything to invite the interest of the authorities. She was an honest woman, according to her lights, an honest wife, and kept an honest house; but she feared the gendarmerie more than the Wrath of God. And by ukase of Government a certain amount of education was compulsory. So Marcel learned among other things to read, and thereby took his first blind step toward salvation.

Reading being the one pastime which could be practiced without making a noise of any sort to attract undesirable attentions, the boy took to it in self-defence. But before long it had become his passion. He read, by stealth, everything that fell into his hands, a weird mélange of newspapers, illustrated Parisian weeklies, magazines, novels: cullings from the debris of guest-chambers.

Before Marcel was eleven he had read *Les Misérables* with intense appreciation.

His reading, however, was not long confined to works in the French language. Now and again some departing guest would leave an English novel in his room, and these Marcel treasured beyond all other books; they seemed to him, in a way, part of his birthright. Secretly he called himself English in those days, because he knew he wasn’t French: that much, at least, he remembered. And he spent long hours poring over the strange words until; at length, they came to seem less strange in his eyes. And then some accident threw his way a small English-French dictionary.

===Chinese===
不过，他吃倒从来不曾亏欠过；至于借睡眠忘却苦难这份奢侈，也总不好不赏给他。

白天，在到了上学年纪之前，他在过道里提心吊胆地玩耍，玩具是将就凑合的：一副愁苦委顿的小身板，每一记突如其来的脚步声都叫他心提到嗓子眼，还处处碍手碍脚，专挡那些 *femmes-de-chambre*（旅馆女仆）的道——这路女仆，同小说里那些热心肠、急性子、心慈面软的侍女没有半点相通。她们到老板娘跟前告他的状，老板娘便应声前来赏他耳光。他很快便练出一种近乎邪门的狡黠，专攻隐没自身的艺术：她一逼近，他便僵得如死了一般，行动起来悄没声息，恍若一缕幽魂。她打旁边经过，他蜷在暗角里一动不动，倒也时常瞒过她的眼睛。可每逢她自以为孑然一身、一抬眼，蓦地撞上那孩子钉在原地、瞪圆双眼的惊惶凝视，总不免怒不可遏。……

他竟能叨光上学，全亏老板娘心头盘踞着一大恐惧——生怕做下什么，招来官府的兴味。照她那套标准，她是个正经女人、正经妻子，开着一家正经店铺；只是她怕宪兵，胜过怕天谴。而奉政府的敕令，若干教育乃是强制。于是马塞尔学会了识字等诸般本领，从而朝着救赎，迈出了自己懵然无知的第一步。

阅读既是唯一一种操练起来不出半点声响、不致招惹祸事的消遣，孩子便出于自卫染上了它。但不久，它便成了他的酷嗜。他偷偷读遍所有落进他手里的东西——报纸、带插图的巴黎画报、杂志、小说，一锅光怪陆离的大杂烩（mélange）：统统是从客房的弃物堆里捡选出来的。

不满十一岁，马塞尔已满怀激赏地读完了《悲惨世界》（*Les Misérables*）。

不过他的阅读，为时不久便不再囿于法语著作。时不时地，哪位临行的客人会在他房里落下一部英文小说，这类书马塞尔看得比什么书都贵重；在他看来，它们多少算是他与生俱来的一份权利。那些日子里，他暗自管自己叫英国人，因为他知道自己不是法国人：至少这一点，他还记得。他一连好几个钟头埋头苦磕那些古怪的字眼，直到它们终于在他眼里显得不再那么古怪。随后机缘凑巧，又给他送来一册小小的英法词典。

===Original===
He was able to read English before he could speak it.

Out of school hours a drudge and scullion, the associate of scullions and their immediate betters, drawn from that caste of loose tongues and looser morals which breeds servants for small hotels, Marcel at eleven (as nearly as his age can be computed) possessed a comprehension of life at once exact, exhaustive and appalling.

Perhaps it was fortunate that he lived without friendship. His concept of womanhood was incarnate in Madame Troyon; so he gave all the hotel women a wide berth. The menservants he suffered in silence when they would permit it; but his nature was so thoroughly disassociated from anything within their experience that they resented him: a circumstance which exposed him to a certain amount of baiting not unlike that which the village idiot receives at the hands of rustic boors﻿—until Marcel learned to defend himself with a tongue which could distil vitriol from the vernacular, and with fists and feet as well. Thereafter he was left severely to himself and glad of it, since it furnished him with just so much more time for reading and dreaming over what he read.

By fifteen he had developed into a long, lank, loutish youth, with a face of extraordinary pallor, a sullen mouth, hot black eyes, and dark hair like a mane, so seldom was it trimmed. He looked considerably older than he was and the slightness of his body was deceptive, disguising a power of sinewy strength. More than this, he could care very handily for himself in a scrimmage: la savate had no secrets from him, and he had picked up tricks from the Apaches quite as effectual as any in the manual of jiujitsu. Paris he knew as you and I know the palms of our hands, and he could converse with the precision of the native-born in any one of the city’s several odd argots.

To these accomplishments he added that of a thoroughly practised petty thief.

His duties were by day those of valet-de-chambre on the third floor; by night he acted as omnibus in the restaurant. For these services he received no pay and less consideration from his employers (who would have been horrified by the suggestion that they countenanced slavery) only his board and a bed in a room scarcely larger, if somewhat better ventilated, than the boudoir-closet from which he had long since been ousted. This room was on the ground floor, at the back of the house, and boasted a small window overlooking a narrow alley.

===Chinese===
他还不会讲英语，就已先能读英语了。

课余之外，他是打杂的苦力兼洗碗小厮，与洗碗工及其直属上司为伍——统统出自那个舌头松、德行更松的阶层，专为大大小小的旅馆孵化仆役。十一岁上（他的年纪也只能约略推算），马塞尔对人生的理解，已然精确、穷尽，而且骇人。

也许，他活着而不识友谊，倒是件幸事。他对「女性」这一概念的化身，就是特鲁瓦老板娘；于是他对旅馆里一切女眷一概敬而远之。男仆们容得下时，他便默默忍受；可他的天性同他们全部阅历里的任何东西都彻底绝缘，以致他们憋着怨气记恨他：这处境叫他挨过一阵子捉弄，同乡下呆子落在庄稼汉手里受的作弄相去无几——直到马塞尔学会用一条能把土话炼出硝镪水的舌头自卫，外加拳打脚踢。此后众人便把他冷冷地撂在一边，他也求之不得：这恰好多给了他光阴去读书，去对着读过的书做梦。

十五岁上，他已出落成一条瘦长笨拙的少年汉子：一张脸白得出奇，一张阴郁的嘴，一双滚烫的黑眼睛，一头鬃毛似的黑发——因为难得修剪。他看上去远比实际年龄苍老，身量的单薄也颇能惑人，掩着一副筋腱虬结的膂力。不止如此，真闹将起来，他照料自己绰有余裕：*la savate*（法式踢打术）对他毫无秘密可言，从阿帕奇（Apaches，巴黎流氓）那里学来的招数，也同柔术教程里的任何一招一样灵验。巴黎，他熟得如你我之于自己的掌纹；这座城通行的几种古怪切口，他说起来个个地道，如假包换的土生土长。

在这些本事之外，他又添上一门：一个千锤百炼的小偷小摸。

白天，他的职分是三楼的客房仆役（valet-de-chambre）；入夜，他就在餐馆里充任全能跑堂。这两份差事换来的，是分文工资皆无、体恤更是负数——东家们（若有人暗示他们纵容奴隶制，准会骇然失色）——只管他食宿，外加底楼后部一个房间里的床位：那屋子比他早先被逐出的闺房小间大不了许多，只是通风稍胜。房间在底层、屋子后身，亏得有一扇小窗，俯临一条窄巷。


===Original===
He was routed out before daylight, and his working day ended as a rule at ten in the evening﻿—though when there were performances on at the Odéon, the restaurant remained open until an indeterminate hour for the accommodation of the supper trade.

Once back in his kennel, its door closed and bolted, Marcel was free to squirm out of the window and roam and range Paris at will. And it was thus that he came by most of his knowledge of the city.

But for the most part Marcel preferred to lie abed and read himself half-blind by the light of purloined candle-ends. Books he borrowed as of old from the rooms of guests or else pilfered from quai-side stalls and later sold to dealers in more distant quarters of the city. Now and again, when he needed some work not to be acquired save through outright purchase, the guests would pay further if unconscious tribute through the sly abstraction of small coins. Your true Parisian, however, keeps track of his money to the ultimate sou, an idiosyncrasy which obliged the boy to practise most of his peculations on the fugitive guest of foreign extraction.

In the number of these, perhaps the one best known to Troyon’s was Bourke.

He was a quick, compact, dangerous little Irishman who had fallen into the habit of “resting” at Troyon’s whenever a vacation from London seemed a prescription apt to prove wholesome for a gentleman of his kidney; which was rather frequently, arguing that Bourke’s professional activities were fairly onerous.

Having received most of his education in Dublin University, Bourke spoke the purest English known, or could when so minded, while his facile Irish tongue had caught the trick of an accent which passed unchallenged on the Boulevardes. He had an alert eye for pretty women, a heart as big as all outdoors, no scruples worth mentioning, a secret sorrow, and a pet superstition.

===Chinese===
天不亮他就被轰起来，一个工作日照例到晚上十点方休——不过每逢奥代翁剧院（Odéon）有戏，餐馆便为照应夜宵生意，开到不知几时。

一旦回到他的狗窝，门一关、闩一插，马塞尔便可自由自在地打窗口钻出去，恣意游荡、纵横巴黎。他对这座城市的大部分知识，正是这般攒下的。

不过大多数时候，马塞尔宁可赖在床上，就着顺来的蜡烛头，把书读到半瞎。书，他照旧从客房里「借」，要不就从滨河书摊上摸走，回头转卖给城里远处坊市的书贩。偶尔，当他需要某部非得掏钱正买不可的书时，客人们便会经由悄然蒸发的小硬币，再付一笔不自知的贡赋。只是你那真正的巴黎人，一文钱都要数到最后一苏（sou，法国小铜币）——这脾性逼得孩子把大部分手脚，改做到来去匆匆的外国过客头上。

这类客人之中，特鲁瓦旅馆最熟悉的，或许要数伯克。

他是个利落、敦实、危险的小个子爱尔兰人，养成了一种习惯：但凡觉得暂离伦敦、出门休养，于他这路绅士似乎是一剂颇有保健之效的处方，他便来特鲁瓦旅馆「休养」。而这类处方开得相当频繁，足见伯克的职业营生相当繁重。

伯克的教养大半得自都柏林大学（Dublin University），说一口人世间最纯正的英语——或者说，他愿意时便说得出；而他那灵便的爱尔兰舌头又习得一口腔调，在林荫大道上通行无阻。他对漂亮女人目光锐利，心胸宽得漫无边际，没什么值得一提的顾忌，有一桩隐痛，还有一件心爱的迷信。

===Original===
The colour of his hair, a clamorous red, was the spring of his secret sorrow. By that token he was a marked man. At irregular intervals he made frantic attempts to disguise it; but the only dye that would serve at all was a jet-black and looked like the devil in contrast with his high colouring. Moreover, before a week passed, the red would crop up again wherever the hair grew thin, lending him the appearance of a badly-singed pup.

His pet superstition was that, as long as he refrained from practising his profession in Paris, Paris would remain his impregnable Tower of Refuge. The world owed Bourke a living, or he so considered; and it must be allowed that he made collections on account with tolerable regularity and success; but Paris was tax-exempt as long as Paris offered him immunity from molestation.

Not only did Paris suit his tastes excellently, but there was no place, in Bourke’s esteem, comparable with Troyon’s for peace and quiet. Hence, the continuity of his patronage was never broken by trials of rival hostelries; and Troyon’s was always expecting Bourke for the simple reason that he invariably arrived unexpectedly, with neither warning nor ostentation, to stop as long as he liked, whether a day or a week or a month, and depart in the same manner.

His daily routine, as Troyon’s came to know it, varied but slightly: he breakfasted abed, about half after ten, lounged in his room or the café all day if the weather were bad, or strolled peacefully in the gardens of the Luxembourg if it were good, dined early and well but always alone, and shortly afterward departed by cab for some well-known bar on the Rive Droit; whence, it is to be presumed, he moved on to other resorts, for he never was home when the house was officially closed for the night, the hours of his return remaining a secret between himself and the concierge.

On retiring, Bourke would empty his pockets upon the dressing-table, where the boy Marcel, bringing up Bourke’s *petit déjeuner* the next morning, would see displayed a tempting confusion of gold and silver and copper, with a wad of banknotes, and the customary assortment of personal hardware.

Now inasmuch as Bourke was never wide-awake at that hour, and always after acknowledging Marcel’s “bon jour” rolled over and snored for Glory and the Saints, it was against human nature to resist the allure of that dressing-table. Marcel seldom departed without a coin or two.

===Chinese===
他那头头发的颜色——一种吵吵嚷嚷的红——便是隐痛之源。凭着这个记号，他成了挂了号的人物。隔三差五，他便发疯似的想法子遮掩；可唯一勉强顶用的染料是漆黑色，衬着他红艳的气色，瞧着活像恶鬼临身。况且不出一个礼拜，但凡头发稀薄之处，红色便又钻出头来，害他一副让火燎得狼狈不堪的小狗模样。

他心爱的迷信是：只要他不曾在巴黎操持本行，巴黎便永远是他固若金汤的避难高塔。世界欠伯克一份生计——至少他自己这般认定；而且必须承认，他为此预收账款，催讨得尚算按部就班、卓有成效；只是巴黎享有豁免，只要巴黎保他不受搅扰。

巴黎不但极合他的口味，而且在伯克看来，论清静安宁，天下没有一处比得上特鲁瓦旅馆。因此，他的光顾从不因试探别家客栈而断档；特鲁瓦旅馆也永远在盼着伯克——道理很简单：他向来不期而至，不打招呼，不事张扬，爱住多久住多久，一天也罢、一礼拜也罢、一个月也罢，走时又如法炮制。

他的日常起居，特鲁瓦旅馆渐渐摸熟了，变动甚微：他在床上用早饭，约摸十点半；天气坏，便整日懒在他房里或咖啡馆；天气好，便去卢森堡公园悠悠然散步；晚饭吃得早而丰盛，却永远独个儿；饭后不久便乘马车赴右岸某家知名酒吧；此后——据推测——再转往别的销金之所，因为旅馆夜里正式落闩之时他从不在店，他归房的时辰，始终是他与门房间守着的秘密。

就寝之前，伯克总要把口袋倾倒在梳妆台上；次日清晨，小马塞尔端着伯克的 *petit déjeuner*（早餐）上楼，便会看见台上摊陈着一堆金银铜币，乱得诱人，外加一沓钞票，以及他惯常携带的那几件私人「五金」。

而在那个钟点，伯克从来不曾清醒过；应过马塞尔一声「bon jour」（您好）之后，他总要翻个身，为荣耀与诸圣徒打起呼噜——要抗拒那张梳妆台的魅惑，实非人性所能。马塞尔离开时，鲜有不捎带一两枚硬币的时候。

===Original===
He had yet to learn that Bourke’s habits were those of an Englishman, who never goes to bed without leaving all his pocket-money in plain sight and﻿—carefully catalogued in his memory.﻿ ﻿…

One morning in the spring of 1904 Marcel served Bourke his last breakfast at Troyon’s.

The Irishman had been on the prowl the previous night, and his rasping snore was audible even through the closed door when Marcel knocked and, receiving no answer, used the passkey and entered.

At this the snore was briefly interrupted; Bourke, visible at first only as a flaming shock of hair protruding from the bedclothes, squirmed an eye above his artificial horizon, opened it, mumbled inarticulate acknowledgment of Marcel’s salutation, and passed blatantly into further slumbers.

Marcel deposited his tray on a table beside the bed, moved quietly to the windows, closed them, and drew the lace curtains together. The dressing-table between the windows displayed, amid the silver and copper, more gold coins than it commonly did﻿—some eighteen or twenty louis altogether. Adroitly abstracting en passant a piece of ten francs, Marcel went on his way rejoicing, touched a match to the fire all ready-laid in the grate, and was nearing the door when, casting one casual parting glance at the bed, he became aware of a notable phenomenon: the snoring was going on lustily, but Bourke was watching him with both eyes wide and filled with interest.

Startled and, to tell the truth, a bit indignant, the boy stopped as though at word of command. But after the first flash of astonishment his young face hardened to immobility. Only his eyes remained constant to Bourke’s.

===Chinese===
他还得再学上一课，方才明白：伯克的习性是彻头彻尾英国式的——英国人就寝之前，必把全部零钱摆在明处，而且——早已在记忆里编目造册。……

1904 年春天的一个早晨，马塞尔在特鲁瓦旅馆伺候了伯克最后一顿早餐。

爱尔兰人前一夜出去「巡猎」了一番，他那锉刀似的呼噜，隔着紧闭的房门都听得见；马塞尔敲了敲门，无人应声，便取万能钥匙开门入内。

这一来，呼噜短暂地中断；伯克起初只现形为一撮火焰似的乱发，自被褥间戳出；他蠕动着将一只眼睛拱上他那个人造地平线，睁开，对马塞尔的问安含混不清地应了一声，便明目张胆地重坠梦乡。

马塞尔把托盘搁上床边的桌子，悄悄走到窗前，关了窗，拉拢花边窗帘。两窗之间的梳妆台上，在银币铜币之间，陈着比往常更多的金币——统共约莫十八到二十枚路易（louis，法国金币）。马塞尔顺路（en passant）灵巧地拈走一枚十法郎，欢欢喜喜地自去赶路，又划火柴点着炉膛里架好现成的柴火，正待出门，朝床上随意投去告别的最后一瞥，却觉察到一桩异象：呼噜依旧打得酣畅，伯克却双眼圆睁，兴味盎然地望着他。

孩子吓了一跳——说实话，还有几分恼——像听到一声口令似的顿住了。但初瞬的惊愕过后，他那张年轻的脸便凝成了一动不动。唯有那双眼睛，依旧与伯克的眼睛对峙不移。


===Original===
The Irishman, sitting up in bed, demanded and received the piece of ten francs, and went on to indict the boy for the embezzlement of several sums running into a number of louis.

Marcel, reflecting that Bourke’s reckoning was still some louis shy, made no bones about pleading guilty. Interrogated, the culprit deposed that he had taken the money because he needed it to buy books. No, he wasn’t sorry. Yes, it was probable that, granted further opportunity, he would do it again. Advised that he was apparently a case-hardened young criminal, he replied that youth was not his fault; with years and experience he would certainly improve.

Puzzled by the boy’s attitude, Bourke agitated his hair and wondered aloud how Marcel would like it if his employers were informed of his peculations.

Marcel looked pained and pointed out that such a course on the part of Bourke would be obviously unfair; the only real difference between them, he explained, was that where he filched a louis Bourke filched thousands; and if Bourke insisted on turning him over to the mercy of Madame and Papa Troyon, who would certainly summon a *sergent de ville*, he, Marcel, would be quite justified in retaliating by telling the *Préfecture de Police* all he knew about Bourke.

This was no chance shot, and took the Irishman between wind and water; and when, dismayed, he blustered, demanding to know what the boy meant by his damned impudence, Marcel quietly advised him that one knew what one knew: if one read the English newspaper in the café, as Marcel did, one could hardly fail to remark that monsieur always came to Paris after some notable burglary had been committed in London; and if one troubled to follow monsieur by night, as Marcel had, it became evident that monsieur’s first calls in Paris were invariably made at the establishment of a famous fence in the rue des Trois Frères; and, finally, one drew one’s own conclusions when strangers dining in the restaurant﻿—as on the night before, by way of illustration﻿—strangers who wore all the hallmarks of police detectives from England﻿—catechised one about a person whose description was the portrait of Bourke, and promised a hundred-franc note for information concerning the habits and whereabouts of that person, if seen.

Marcel added, while Bourke gasped for breath, that the gentleman in question had spoken to him alone, in the absence of other waiters, and had been fobbed off with a lie.

===Chinese===
爱尔兰人在床上坐起身，索回并收下了那枚十法郎，随即提起控诉，告这孩子陆续侵吞数笔款项，累至若干枚路易。

马塞尔盘算着，伯克的账目尚短几个路易未计，便毫不扭捏地俯首认罪。一经盘问，罪犯供称：他拿那些钱，是因为需要买书。不，他不觉得抱歉。是，倘若再有机会，他多半还会再犯。有人开导他，说他显然已是名屡教不改的少年罪犯；他答道：年少非他之过；再添些年岁与阅历，他必有长进。

孩子的态度把伯克弄糊涂了；他抓挠得头发乱动，朗声纳罕：马塞尔的东家若得知他那些手脚，他猜马塞尔将作何感想。

马塞尔面有愠色，指出伯克果真出此一策，显然有失公道；两人之间唯一真正的区别，他解释道，不过在于他摸一个路易的地方，伯克摸的是成千上万；倘若伯克执意要将他交由特鲁瓦老板和老板娘发落——他们必定会唤来一名 *sergent de ville*（巡警）——那么他马塞尔便完全有正当理由还手：把他所知有关伯克的一切，尽数说给警察总局（*Préfecture de Police*）听。

这一枪绝非碰巧，正打在爱尔兰人的水线腰眼上；他着了慌，咆哮起来，质问那孩子这该死的放肆是何用意；马塞尔便心平气和地开导他：知道的事，终归是知道的：人若在咖啡馆里读英文报纸——马塞尔就读——便不难留意到：每逢伦敦出了桩了不得的盗窃案，「先生」（monsieur）总要随后驾临巴黎；人若不辞辛苦，夜里尾随先生一程——马塞尔就尾随过——便一目了然：先生在巴黎的头几处拜会，回回落在三兄弟街（rue des Trois Frères）一位大名鼎鼎的销赃人铺面；末了，当生客在餐馆里——姑且以前夜为例——盘问起一个人的底细，那生客浑身披挂着英格兰警探的种种印记，所问之人的相貌恰是伯克的写照，还许下一张一百法郎的票子，悬赏此人的习性与去向，只要有人得见——到这一步，人自然得出人的结论。

马塞尔又补上一句——此时伯克正喘不上气来——那位先生当时是单独找的他，避开了旁的侍者，而且已经被一句谎话打发掉了。

===Original===
But why﻿—Bourke wanted to know﻿—had Marcel lied to save him, when the truth would have earned him a hundred francs?

“Because,” Marcel explained coolly, “I, too, am a thief. Monsieur will perceive it was a matter of professional honour.”

Now the Irish have their faults, but ingratitude is not of their number.

Bourke, packing hastily to leave Paris, France and Europe by the fastest feasible route, still found time to question Marcel briefly; and what he learned from the boy about his antecedents so worked with gratitude upon the sentimental nature of the Celt, that when on the third day following the Cunarder *Carpathia* left Naples for New York, she carried not only a gentleman whose brilliant black hair and glowing pink complexion rendered him a bit too conspicuous among her first-cabin passengers for his own comfort, but also in the second cabin his valet﻿—a boy of sixteen who looked eighteen.

The gentleman’s name on the passenger-list didn’t, of course, in the least resemble Bourke. His valet’s was given as Michael Lanyard.

The origin of this name is obscure; Michael being easily corrupted into good Irish Mickey may safely be attributed to Bourke; Lanyard has a tang of the sea which suggests a reminiscence of some sea-tale prized by the pseudo Marcel Troyon.

===Chinese===
可是——伯克想要知道——实话说出来便能赚一百法郎，马塞尔偏要撒谎救他，却是为何？

「因为，」马塞尔冷冷地解释，「我也是一个贼。先生当能明察：这是行当体面攸关之事。」

爱尔兰人固然毛病成筐，忘恩负义却不在其中。

伯克手忙脚乱地收拾行李，只求以最快捷可行的路线离开巴黎、离开法国、离开欧洲，却仍挤出工夫，把马塞尔简短地盘问了一番；他从孩子口中听来的那段身世，同感激之情一齐，在那凯尔特人多愁善感的本性里发酵；于是三天过后，当冠达公司的「卡尔帕提亚号」（*Carpathia*）邮轮驶离那不勒斯（Naples）、航向纽约时，船上载着的不止一位绅士——他那一头油亮的黑发、灼灼焕发的红润脸色，使他置身头等舱旅客之间未免太扎眼，叫他自己也不自在——二等舱里还载着他的贴身仆人：一个十六岁、看着像十八岁的少年。

旅客名单上，那位绅士的大名，自然与「伯克」毫无相似之处。他的仆人则登记为：迈克尔·兰亚德。

这名字的来历已不可考；Michael 既然极易讹作地道爱尔兰的 Mickey，尽可以归功于伯克；Lanyard 却带着一股海水味，令人推想，是那伪托的马塞尔·特鲁瓦所珍藏的某篇海上奇谈留下的余韵。

===Original===
In New York began the second stage in the education of a professional criminal. The boy must have searched far for a preceptor of more sound attainments than Bourke. It is, however, only fair to say that Bourke must have looked as far for an apter pupil. Under his tutelage, Michael Lanyard learned many things; he became a mathematician of considerable promise, an expert mechanician, a connoisseur of armour-plate and explosives in their more pacific applications, and he learned to grade precious stones with a glance. Also, because Bourke was born of gentlefolk, he learned to speak English, what clothes to wear and when to wear them, and the civilized practice with knife and fork at table. And because Bourke was a diplomatist of sorts, Marcel acquired the knack of being at ease in every grade of society: he came to know that a self-made millionaire, taken the right way, is as approachable as one whose millions date back even unto the third generation; he could order a dinner at Sherry’s as readily as drinks at Sharkey’s. Most valuable accomplishment of all, he learned to laugh. In the way of byproducts he picked up a working acquaintance with American, English and German slang﻿—French slang he already knew as a mother-tongue﻿—considerable geographical knowledge of the capitals of Europe, America and Illinois, a taste that discriminated between tobacco and the stuff sold as such in France, and a genuine passion for good paintings.

Finally Bourke drilled into his apprentice the three cardinal principles of successful cracksmanship: to know his ground thoroughly before venturing upon it; to strike and retreat with the swift precision of a hawk; to be friendless.

And the last of these was the greatest.

“You’re a promising lad,” he said﻿—so often that Lanyard would almost wince from that formula of introduction﻿—“a promising lad, though it’s sad I should be to say it, instead of proud as I am. For I’ve made you: but for me you’d long since have matriculated at La Tour Pointue and graduated with the canaille of the Santé. And in time you may become a first-chop operator, which I’m not and never will be; but if you do, ’twill be through fighting shy of two things. The first of them’s Woman, and the second is Man. To make a friend of a man you must lower your guard. Ordinarily ’tis fatal. As for Woman, remember this, m’lad: to let love into your life you must open a door no mortal hand can close. And God only knows what’ll follow in. If ever you find you’ve fallen in love and can’t fall out, cut the game on the instant, or you’ll end wearing stripes or broad arrows﻿—the same as myself would, if this cursed cough wasn’t going to be the death of me.﻿ ﻿… No, m’lad: take a fool’s advice (you’ll never get better) and when you’re shut of me, which will be soon, I’m thinking, take the Lonesome Road and stick to the middle of it. ‘He travels the fastest that travels alone’ is a true saying, but ’tis only half the truth: he travels the farthest into the bargain.﻿ ﻿… Yet the Lonesome Road has its drawbacks, lad﻿—it’s *damned* lonely!”

Bourke died in Switzerland, of consumption, in the winter of 1910﻿—Lanyard at his side till the end.

Then the boy set his face against the world: alone, lonely, and remembering.

===Chinese===
职业罪犯教育的第二阶段，始于纽约。这孩子为寻一位造诣比伯克更扎实的业师，想必已是踏破铁鞋。不过公道地讲，伯克为寻一个更堪雕琢的学生，想必也踏破过铁鞋。在他的调教之下，迈克尔·兰亚德学会了诸多本领：他成了前程远大的数学家、娴熟的机械师、装甲钢板与炸药较为和平用途上的行家，还学会一瞥之间定宝石的品级。又因伯克出身良家，他学会了讲英语，学会了穿什么衣裳、何时穿，学会了餐桌上刀叉的文明仪轨。又因伯克算得半个外交家，马塞尔练出了在任一等社会阶层里皆如鱼得水的本领：他明白，白手起家的百万富翁，只要拿捏得法，同家资直溯三代的百万富翁一般可以亲近；他在谢里餐馆（Sherry's）点一桌正餐，能像在沙基酒馆（Sharkey's）叫一杯酒一样轻而易举。而万般造诣之中最可贵的，是他学会了笑。至于副产品，他顺手拾得了美、英、德三国俚语的实用知识——法国俚语他早已视同母语——对欧洲、美洲与伊利诺伊（Illinois）诸都城颇具地理上的造诣，养成了一种能分辨烟草与法国市面冒牌货的口味，还生出了对好画的一片真心热忱。

最后，伯克向徒弟灌输了撬保险柜这一行的三条成功要诀：一、动手之前，务必彻底踩点；二、出手与撤退，务求鹰隼般迅疾精准；三、孤身无友。

而其中最后一条，最是要紧。

「你是个有出息的小子，」他说——说得次数之多，害兰亚德一听这句引荐的套语便几乎要皱眉——「有出息的小子，虽说讲出来叫我心酸，而不能像我本当的那样骄傲。因为是我造就了你：若没有我，你早在『尖塔』（La Tour Pointue）注册入学，同桑泰监狱（the Santé）的流氓渣滓一道毕业了。假以时日，你兴许能成为头一流的作案高手——我就不是，也永远成不了；可你若真成了，那必是靠躲开两样东西。头一样，是女人；第二样，是男人。要与一个男人交朋友，你就得放下戒备。寻常这可致命。至于女人，记住这个，小子：要让爱情进你的生活，你就得打开一扇门，一只凡人之手也关不上的门。而天知道随后会跟进些什么来。哪天你发现自己堕入情网又拔不出来，就立马金盆洗手，不然你到头来不是身披条纹囚衣，便是烙上宽箭记号——跟我一个下场，若不是这该死的咳嗽要先拿我去见上帝的话。……不，小子：听傻瓜一句劝（你再听不着更好的了），等你甩脱了我——我想时日无多了——就走『孤独之路』（the Lonesome Road），拣正中间走。『独行者行得最快』，这话不假，可它只说了一半的真：他还行得最远呢。……只是『孤独之路』也有它的难处，孩子——它*天杀的*太孤独了！」

1910 年冬天，伯克殁于瑞士，殁于痨病——兰亚德守在榻畔，直到最后一刻。

于是，那少年掉转身来，与整个世界相抗：独自一人，孤独无依，且铭记不忘。

## X Turn About / 十、攻守易势

===Original===
The thought of Lanyard’s pocket flash-lamp offering itself, immediately its wide circle of light enveloped his late antagonist.
That one was resting on a shoulder, legs uncouthly a-sprawl, quite without movement of any perceptible sort; his face more than half-turned to the floor, and masked into the bargain.
Incredulously Lanyard stirred the body with a foot, holding his weapon poised as though half-expecting it to quicken with instant and violent action; but it responded in no way.
With a nod of satisfaction, he shifted the light until it marked down the nearest electric bulb, which proved, in line with his inference, to have been extinguished by the socket key, while the heat of its bulb indicated that the current had been shut off only an instant before his entrance.
The light full up, he went back to the thug, knelt and, lifting the body, turned it upon its back.

===Chinese===
兰亚德心念方动，袖珍手电便自己凑了上来，一圈宽阔的光晕旋即罩住了他方才的对手。
那家伙一侧肩膀支着地，两腿不成体统地八字摊开，丝毫不见可辨的动静；脸大半扭向地板，外加还罩着面罩。
兰亚德将信将疑，伸脚碰了碰那躯体，手里的枪仍端得稳稳的，仿佛半指望它会猝然奋起、大动干戈；可它毫无回应。
他满意地点了点头，把光挪过去，锁定最近的那只电灯泡：不出所料，那灯正是叫灯座开关拧灭的，而灯泡的余热表明，断电不过发生在他进门之前一瞬。
灯火通明之后，他回到那恶棍跟前，跪下一条腿，托起躯体，把它翻了个仰面朝天。

===Original===
Recognition immediately rewarded this manoeuvre: the masked face upturned to the glare was that of the American who had made a fourth in the concert of the Pack﻿—“Mr. Smith.”
Quickly unlatching the mask, Lanyard removed it; but the countenance thus exposed told little more than he knew; he could have sworn he had never seen it before. None the less, something in its evil cast persistently troubled his memory, with the same provoking and baffling effect that had attended their first encounter.
Already the American was struggling toward consciousness. His lips and eyelids twitched spasmodically, he shuddered, and his flexed muscles began to relax. In this process something fell from between the fingers of his right hand﻿—something small and silver-bright that caught Lanyard’s eye.
Picking it up, he examined with interest a small hypodermic syringe loaded to the full capacity of its glass cylinder, plunger drawn back﻿—all ready for instant service.
It was the needle of this instrument that had pricked the skin of Lanyard’s neck; beyond reasonable doubt it contained a soporific, if not exactly a killing dose of some narcotic drug﻿—cocaine, at a venture.

===Chinese===
这一着立刻得了认人的报偿：仰对着强光的那张蒙面脸，正是群狼合奏里充当第四位的那名美国人——「史密斯先生」。
兰亚德飞快地解下面罩搭扣，将它摘了下来；可这样亮出来的面孔，也只比他已知道的略多一分；他可以起誓，这张脸生平从未见过。尽管如此，那副凶相里有种什么，偏在他记忆里纠缠不休，同他们初次交手时一样，撩人又惑人。
那美国人已经在朝清醒里挣扎了。他的嘴唇和眼皮一阵阵地痉挛，打了个寒噤，紧绷的肌肉开始松弛下来。就在这个当口，有件东西从他右手的指缝间掉了下去——一件小小的、泛着银光的东西，撞进了兰亚德眼里。
他拾将起来，饶有兴味地察看：一支小小的皮下注射器，玻璃管里的药水灌得满满当当，活塞拉在待发之位——一切收拾停当，只等随时伺候。
在兰亚德颈子上扎那一针的，正是这件家什的针尖；毋庸置疑，管里装的是安眠药——即便算不上恰恰致命的分量，也是某种麻醉毒品——姑且猜它是可卡因。

===Original===
So it appeared that this agent of the Pack had been commissioned to put the Lone Wolf to sleep for an hour or two or more﻿—*perhaps* not permanently!﻿—that he might be out of the way long enough for their occult purposes.
He smiled grimly, fingering the hypodermic and eyeing the prostrate man.
“Turn about,” he reflected, “is said to be fair play.﻿ ﻿… Well, why not?”
He bent forward, dug the needle into the wrist of the American and shot the plunger home, all in a single movement so swift and deft that the drug was delivered before the pain could startle the victim from his coma.
As for that, the man came to quickly enough; but only to have his clearing senses met and dashed by the muzzle of a pistol stamping a cold ring upon his temple.

===Chinese===
如此看来，群狼派这位干将办的事，就是送独狼睡上一两个钟头，或者更久——*兴许*还不至于一睡不醒！——好教他在足够久的工夫里，碍不着他们的暗勾当。
他狞笑一声，指间捻着注射器，眼睛打量着那俯卧的人。
「攻守易势，」他暗自寻思，「据说才算公平交易。……喏，有何不可？」
他俯身向前，针尖刺入美国人的腕子，活塞一推到底——整套动作一气呵成，又快又巧，没等疼痛把那昏死的人惊动分毫，药水已然注射了个干净。
说到醒，那人倒是醒得挺快；只可惜刚回转的一点清明，迎头便被一管枪口浇了个透心凉——枪口抵着他的太阳穴，压出一圈冰冷的环印。

===Original===
“Lie perfectly quiet, my dear Mr. Smith,” Lanyard advised; “don’t speak above a whisper! Give the good dope a chance: it’ll only need a moment, or I’m no judge and you’re a careless highbinder! I’d like to know, however﻿—if it’s all the same to you﻿—”
But already the injection was taking effect; the look of panic, which had drawn the features of the American and flickered from his eyes with dawning appreciation of his plight, was clouding, fading, blending into one of daze and stupour. The eyelids flickered and lay still; the lips moved as if with urgent desire to speak, but were dumb; a long convulsive sigh shook the American’s body; and he rested with the immobility of the dead, save for the slow but steady rise and fall of his bosom.
Lanyard thoughtfully reviewed these phenomena.
“Must kick like a mule, that dope!” he reflected. “Lucky it didn’t get me before I guessed what was up! If I’d even suspected its strength, however, I’d have been less hasty: I could do with a little information from Mr. Mysterious Stranger here!”
Suddenly conscious of a dry and burning throat, he rose and going to the washstand drank deep and thirstily from a water-bottle; then set himself resolutely to repair the disarray of his wits and consider what was best to be done.

===Chinese===
「安安静静躺着，亲爱的史密斯先生，」兰亚德好言劝导，「说话不许高过耳语！给这剂好药一点工夫：只消片刻便见功效——不然我就不配称行家，你也算不得个细心的职业凶手！不过我倒想请教一句——要是您不嫌弃的话——」
可注射这时已经发作了。那副惊惶之色——它曾绷紧美国人的五官，又随着他渐悟自身处境而从眼底闪出——正蒙上一层雾，消退，融成一片迷惘与昏沉。眼皮颤了颤便归于静止；嘴唇翕动，仿佛有满腔急话不吐不快，却哑然无声；一声悠长痉挛的叹息摇过美国人的躯体；他便带着死人才有的纹丝不动歇下了，只余胸脯缓慢而匀净的起伏。
兰亚德若有所思，把这番光景检阅了一遍。
「这药水的劲道，赶得上骡子尥蹶子！」他暗想。「亏得没等我省悟出蹊跷，就叫它放倒了！不过话说回来，哪怕只疑心到它的霸道三分，我也不至于这样毛手毛脚：这位神秘陌生客先生肚里的货色，我还想讨教一二呢！」
他猛然觉得喉咙干得冒火，便起身走到洗脸架前，就着水壶痛痛快快灌了几大口；然后打定主意，定要收拾起方才乱作一团的神志，好好盘算眼下何去何从。

===Original===
In his abstraction he wandered to a chair over whose back hung a light dressing-gown of wine-coloured silk, which, because it would pack in small compass, was in the habit of carrying with him on his travels. Lanyard had left this thrown across his bed; and he was wondering subconsciously what use the man had thought to make of it, that he should have taken the trouble to shift it to the chair.
But even as he laid hold of it, Lanyard dropped the garment in sheer surprise to find it damp and heavy in his grasp, sodden with viscid moisture. And when, in a swift flash of intuition, he examined his fingers, he discovered them discoloured with a faint reddish stain.
Had the dye run? And how had the American come to dabble the garment in water﻿—to what end?
Then the shape of an object on the floor near his feet arrested Lanyard’s questing vision. He stared, incredulous, moved forward, bent over and picked it up, clipping it gingerly between fingertips.
It was one of his razors﻿—a heavy hollow-ground blade﻿—and it was foul with blood.

===Chinese===
心神恍惚间，他踱到一把椅子跟前；椅背上搭着一件酒红色绸子的轻薄晨衣——这衣裳叠起来不占地方，是他出门旅行带惯了的。兰亚德原是将它撂在床上的；此刻潜意识里正纳闷：那人劳神把它挪到椅子上，是想派个什么用场。
可他刚一沾手，便惊得撒手放脱了那件晨衣——入手又湿又沉，浸透了黏腻的水气。紧接着直觉如电光一闪，他察看自己的手指，只见指头上染着一层淡淡的红色渍痕。
是绸子的染料洇了么？那美国人又怎会想起来把这件衣裳浸到水里——所为何来？
便在此时，脚边地板上一件东西的轮廓，截住了兰亚德逡巡四顾的目光。他瞪眼望着，难信其为真，上前俯身拾起，只以指尖小心翼翼地拈着。
那是他的一把剃刀——沉沉一片凹磨的刀刃——上面血污狼藉。

===Original===
With a low cry, smitten with awful understanding, Lanyard wheeled and stared fearfully at the door communicating with Roddy’s room.
It stood ajar an inch or two, its splintered lock accounted for by a small but extremely efficient jointed steel jimmy which lay near the threshold.
Beyond the door﻿ ﻿… darkness﻿ ﻿… silence﻿ ﻿…
Mustering up all his courage, the adventurer strode determinedly into the adjoining room.
The first flash of his hand-lamp discovered to him sickening verification of his most dreadful apprehensions.

===Chinese===
兰亚德低呼一声，被那可怕的领悟当胸击中，霍然回身，满怀惊怖地死盯住通往罗迪房间的那扇连通门。
门虚开着一两英寸宽；门锁何以崩裂了木茬，也有了交代——门槛近旁躺着一把小巧而极其得用的折叠钢撬棍。
门的那一边……是黑暗……是沉寂……
这位冒险家鼓足了全部勇气，大步决然地跨进隔壁房间。
手电第一闪，便给了他一个令人作呕的证实——他最不祥的预感，分毫不爽。

===Original===
Now he saw why his dressing-gown had been requisitioned﻿—to protect a butcher’s clothing.
After a moment he returned, shut the door, and set his back against it, as if to bar out that reeking shambles.
He was very pale, his face drawn with horror; and he was powerfully shaken with nausea.
The plot was damnably patent: Roddy proving a menace to the Pack and requiring elimination, his murder had been decreed as well as that the blame for it should be laid at Lanyard’s door. Hence the attempt to drug him, that he might not escape before police could be sent to find him there.

===Chinese===
如今他明白晨衣缘何被征用了——为的是罩住屠夫的衣裳。
半晌，他退了回来，关上门，背脊抵住门扇，仿佛要把那血气蒸腾的屠场关在门外。
他面无人色，一张脸叫恐怖绞得变了形；一阵翻肠搅肚的恶心，把他撼得站立不稳。
这条毒计昭然若揭：罗迪既成了群狼的威胁、非除之不可，那么不但他的死已定了案，罪名也一并议定，要安到兰亚德头上。所以才要用药放倒他，好教他脱身不得，专候警察上门拿个正着。

===Original===
He could no longer doubt that De Morbihan had been left behind at the Circle of Friends of Harmony solely to detain him, if need be, and afford Smith time to finish his hideous job and set the trap for the second victim.
And the plot had succeeded despite its partial failure, despite the swift reverse chance and Lanyard’s cunning had meted out to the Pack’s agent. It was *his* dressing-gown that was saturate with Roddy’s blood, just as they were his gloves, pilfered from his luggage, which had measurably protected the killer’s hands, and which Lanyard had found in the next room, stripped hastily off and thrown to the floor﻿—twin crumpled wads of bloodstained chamois-skin.
He had now little choice; he must either flee Paris and trust to his wits to save him, or else seek De Morbihan and solicit his protection, his boasted influence in high quarters.
But to give himself into the hands, to become an associate, of one who could be party to so cowardly a crime as this﻿ ﻿… Lanyard told himself he would sooner pay the guillotine the penalty.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
他再无从怀疑：德·莫尔比昂之所以被留在「人类之友会」，单单就是为了在必要时绊住他，给史密斯腾出工夫，干完那桩狰狞的勾当，再给第二个受难者布好陷阱。
而这毒计尽管折了半阵，尽管机运骤转、兰亚德的狡狯叫群狼的爪牙吃了迅雷不及的亏，终究还是得逞了。浸透罗迪鲜血的，是*他的*晨衣；正如那副从行李里摸走的、多少护住了凶手双手的手套，也是他的——兰亚德在隔壁屋里见到了它们，被仓皇褪下、掷在地上——两团揉皱的、血渍斑斑的麂皮。
他如今已没有多少可挑的：要么逃出巴黎，把身家性命托付给自家机智；要么去找德·莫尔比昂，求他的庇护，求他向来夸口的、通天的门路。
可是把自己交到这样一个人手里、与他结为同伙——一个能对如此怯懦的罪行甘居同谋的人……兰亚德告诉自个儿：宁可上断头台去付那笔账。……

===Original===
Consulting his watch, he found the hour to be no later than half-past four: so swiftly (truly treading upon one another’s heels) events had moved since the incident of the somnambulist.
This left at his disposal a fair two hours more of darkness: November nights are long and black in Paris; it would hardly be even moderately light before seven o’clock. But that were a respite none too long for Lanyard’s necessity; he must think swiftly in contemplation of instant action were he to extricate himself without the Pack’s knowledge and consent.
Granted, then, he must fly this stricken field of Paris. But how? De Morbihan had promised that Popinot’s creatures would guard every outlet; and Lanyard didn’t doubt him. An attempt to escape the city by any ordinary channel would be to invite either denunciation to the police on the charge of murder, or one of those fatally expeditious forms of assassination of which the Apaches are past-masters.
He must and would find another way; but his decision was frightfully hampered by lack of ready money; the few odd francs in his pocket were no store for the war-chest demanded by this emergency.

===Chinese===
掏出表来一看，时刻不过四点半：自那桩梦游者的变故以来，诸事推进竟如此神速（真真是接踵而至，一步踩着一步的脚跟）。
这便给他余下整整两个钟头的黑暗好使：巴黎的十一月，夜长而黑；不到七点，天色连将亮未亮都谈不上。但对兰亚德的急难而言，这点宽限也实在算不得宽绰；他若要瞒过群狼的耳目、不劳他们点头便脱身事外，那就必须念动如飞、想到即行。
那么，认了：他必须撤出巴黎这片劫后的战场。可是怎么走？德·莫尔比昂放过话，波皮诺的爪牙会把守每一路出口；兰亚德也不疑这话有假。想循寻常路子出城，无异于自请二选一的款待：要么吃警方一纸谋杀的告发，要么领教阿帕奇们那种干净利落、直取性命的暗杀手段——那原是他们的独门绝学。
另一条路，他必须找，也一定找得到；只是他的决断被囊中空空狠狠掣肘：口袋里零零碎碎几个法郎，撑不起这场急难索要的军资。

===Original===
True, he had the Omber jewels; but they were not negotiable﻿—not at least in Paris.
And the Huysman plans?
He pondered briefly the possibilities of the Huysman plans.
In his fretting, pacing softly to and fro, at each turn he passed his dressing-table, and chancing once to observe himself in its mirror, he stopped short, thunderstruck by something he thought to detect in the counterfeit presentment of his countenance, heavy with fatigue as it was, and haggard with contemplation of this appalling contretemps.

===Chinese===
不错，他手里有欧姆贝夫人的珍珠；可那不是能脱手的货——至少在巴黎脱不了手。
那么于伊斯曼图纸呢？
于伊斯曼图纸的种种用场，他短短地运了一回神。
他心焦起来，放轻脚步来回踱着，每一转折都打梳妆台前经过；偶然一遭，他在镜中照见自己，猛可里立定——仿佛从那副尊容的摹写里窥见了什么，惊得他如遭雷击：那张脸本已倦容沉重，又因筹度这桩骇人的 contretemps（法语：意外的变故）而憔悴不堪。

===Original===
And instantly he was back beside the American, studying narrowly the contours of that livid mask. Here, then, was that resemblance which had baffled him; and now that he saw it, he could not deny that it was unflatteringly close: feature for feature the face of the murderer reproduced his face, coarsened perhaps but recognizably a replica of that Michael Lanyard who confronted him every morning in his shaving-glass, almost the only difference residing in the scrubby black moustache that shadowed the American’s upper lip.
After all, there was nothing wonderful in this; Lanyard’s type was not uncommon; he would never have thought himself a distinguished figure.
Before rising he turned out the pockets of his counterfeit. But this profited him little: the assassin had dressed for action with forethought to evade recognition in event of accident. Lanyard collected only a cheap American watch in a rolled-gold case of a sort manufactured by wholesale, a briquet, a common key that might fit any hotel door, a broken paper of Régie cigarettes, an automatic pistol, a few francs in silver﻿—nothing whatever that would serve as a mark of identification; for though the grey clothing was tailor-made, the maker’s labels had been ripped out of its pockets, while the man’s linen and underwear alike lacked even a laundry’s hieroglyphic.
With this harvest of nothing for his pains, Lanyard turned again to the washstand and his shaving kit, mixed a stiff lather, stropped another razor to the finest edge he could manage, fetched a pair of keen scissors from his dressing-case, and went back to the murderer.

===Chinese===
转瞬间他已回到那美国人身边，细细研读那张青灰面具的轮廓。原来如此——搅了他一路的那份相像，根子在此；如今既已看破，他便无法否认，这份相像近得叫人脸上挂不住：五官对五官，凶手的脸复刻着他的脸，兴许粗陋了几分，却分明就是每早清晨在剃须镜里同他打照面的那个迈克尔·兰亚德；仅有的差别，差不多只在美国人上唇掩着的那一撮乱蓬蓬的黑髭。
说到底，这也无甚可奇；兰亚德这副皮相本非稀罕款式；他从不曾自命为出众的人物。
起身之前，他把这具「替身」的口袋翻了个遍。可进项寥寥：那刺客行事早有成算，连万一失手如何免于指认都盘算到了。兰亚德拢共只收得：一只廉价美国表，批发出品的包金壳；一只打火机（briquet）；一把寻常钥匙，兴许配得上任何一家旅馆的门；一包拆开了的专卖局（Régie）香烟；一支自动手枪；几枚银法郎——没有一样东西堪充指认的凭据；那身灰衣裳虽是裁缝定做，口袋里的店家字号却统统撕了去；此君的衬衫内衣也一概欠奉，连洗衣坊的密码记号都没有半个。
这一场劳而无功的丰收过后，兰亚德又回到洗脸架和剃须家什跟前，打了一碗稠厚的皂沫，另取一把剃刀，在磨刀布上勒出他所能勒出的最锋的刃口，再从化妆盒里取出一把快剪，回到那凶手身边。

===Original===
He worked rapidly, at a high pitch of excitement﻿—as much through sheer desperation as through any appeal inherent in the scheme either to his commonsense or to his romantic bent.
In two minutes he had stripped the moustache clean away from that stupid, flaccid mask.
Unquestionably the resemblance was now most striking; the American would readily pass for Michael Lanyard.
This much accomplished, he pursued his preparations in feverish haste. In spite of this, he overlooked no detail. In less than twenty minutes he had exchanged clothing with the American in detail, even down to shirts, collars and neckties; had packed in his own pockets the several articles taken from the other, together with the jointed jimmy and a few of his personal effects, and was ready to bid adieu to himself, to that Michael Lanyard whom Paris knew.
The insentient masquerader on the floor had called himself “good-enough Smith”; he must serve now as good-enough Lanyard, at least for the Lone Wolf’s purposes; the police at all events would accept him as such. And if the memory of Michael Lanyard must needs wear the stigma of brutal murder, he need not repine in his oblivion, since through this perfunctory decease the Lone Wolf would gain a freedom even greater than before.

===Chinese===
他干活干得飞快，兴奋得紧弦高吊——一半是绝境里逼出来的孤注，一半才是这计划本身对他常识的召唤，或是对他浪漫脾性的投合。
两分钟工夫，那撇胡子已从那张呆滞松垂的面具上刮了个一干二净。
无可置疑，此刻的相像已臻极致；这美国人冒充迈克尔·兰亚德，一冒一个准儿。
这一步办妥，他便热病一般火速继续筹备。尽管如此，他没有漏掉任何细节。不到二十分钟，他已同那美国人通身换了行头，连衬衫、领子、领带都换到了家；又把从对方身上取来的那几件零碎，连同折叠撬棍和几件随身私物，一并装进自家口袋，就此预备同他自己道别——同那个巴黎所熟识的迈克尔·兰亚德，道一声 adieu（法语：永别）。
地板上那位无知无觉的假面客，自称过「尽够用」史密斯；如今他得屈就「尽够用」兰亚德，至少在独狼的用场上尽够一用；无论如何，警察是会照单全收的。而倘若迈克尔·兰亚德的名声注定要披上凶残谋杀的污名，他在九泉之下也无须怨叹，因为借这一场敷衍塞责的死亡，独狼将赢得比从前更阔大的自由。

===Original===
The Pack had contrived only to eliminate Michael Lanyard, the amateur of fine paintings; remained the Lone Wolf with not one faculty impaired, but rather with a deadlier purpose to shape his occult courses.﻿ ﻿…
Under the influence of his methodical preparations, his emotions had cooled appreciably, taking on a cast of cold malignant vengefulness.
He who never in all his criminal record had so much as pulled trigger in self-defence, was ready now to shoot to kill with the most cold-blooded intent﻿—given one of three targets; while Popinot’s creatures, if they worried him, he meant to exterminate with as little compunction as though they were rats in fact as well as in spirit.﻿ ﻿…
Extinguishing the lights, he stepped quickly to a window and from one edge of its shade looked down into the street.

===Chinese===
群狼算计成的，不过是剪除迈克尔·兰亚德这位精鉴名画的业余雅士；留下来的独狼，一样本领不曾折损，反倒多了一副更致命的心肠，好去开辟他那些隐秘的路数。……
经了这番有条不紊的筹备的熏陶，他的心绪已明显冷却下来，凝出一层冷酷、歹毒、专事寻仇的成色。
他这个人，犯罪史上连自卫都未曾扣过一回扳机，如今却横了最冷血的心，瞄上三个目标中的任何一个便要开枪取命；至于波皮诺的爪牙，胆敢来缠他，他打算剿杀起来不动一点恻隐，只当它们是形神俱备的真耗子。……
熄了灯，他快步踱到一扇窗前，从窗帘一边的缝隙里俯瞰街道。

===Original===
He was in time to see a stunted human silhouette detach itself from the shadow of a doorway on the opposite walk, move to the curb, and wave an arm﻿—evidently signaling another sentinel on a corner out of Lanyard’s range of vision.
Herein was additional proof, if any lacked, that De Morbihan had not exaggerated the disposition of Popinot. This animal in the street, momentarily revealed by the corner light as he darted across to take position by the door, this animal with sickly face and pointed chin, with dirty muffler round its chicken-neck, shoddy coat clothing its sloping shoulders, baggy corduroy trousers flapping round its bony shanks﻿—this was Popinot’s, and but one of a thousand differing in no essential save degree of viciousness.
It wasn’t possible to guess how thoroughly Popinot had picketed the house, in cooperation with Roddy’s murderer, by way of provision against mischance; but the adventurer was satisfied that, in his proper guise as himself, he needed only to open that postern door at the street end of the passage, to feel a knife slip in between his ribs﻿—most probably in his back, beneath the shoulder-blade.﻿ ﻿…
He nodded grimly, moved back from the window, and used the flash-lamp to light him to the door.

===Chinese===
他赶得正巧：对面人行道上一处门洞的暗影里，一个矮墩墩的人影分离出来，走到路缘，挥了挥手臂——分明是在给另一名哨兵打信号，那人守在兰亚德视野之外的街角上。
此间又添一证——倘若还嫌证据不足的话——德·莫尔比昂对波皮诺布防的种种，并无半字夸张。街上那头畜生，蹿过街去、抢到门边占位时，被街角的灯光霎时照亮：一张病黄的脸、一个尖尖的下巴，鸡脖子上缠一条脏围巾，软塌塌的外套披在溜肩膀上，宽宽松松的灯芯绒裤在细瘦的腿杆上扑扇——这便是波皮诺的畜生，而且是千百头里的一头，除了歹毒的程度深浅，本质上半点不差。
波皮诺同罗迪的凶手如何联手、把这所房子钉了多密的桩子以防不虞，猜是猜不透的；但这位冒险家确信：他若还以本来面目出门，只消推开通道临街一端那扇后门，便准会觉出一柄刀滑进肋骨之间——十之八九从背后、肩胛骨底下。……
他冷然点了点头，从窗前退开，打开手电照着路，向房门走去。

## XIX Unmasked / 十九、卸下面具

===Original===
When there was no more light in the sky, a profound sigh escaped Lanyard’s lips; and with the gesture of one signifying submission to an omen, he turned and tramped heavily back across-town.
More automaton than sentient being, he plodded on along the second enceinte of flaring, noisy boulevards, now and again narrowly escaping annihilation beneath the wheels of some coursing motor-cab or ponderous, grinding omnibus.
Barely conscious of such escapes, he was altogether indifferent to them: it would have required a mortal hurt to match the dumb, sick anguish of his soul; more than merely a sunset sky had turned black for him within that hour.
The cold was now intense, and he none too warmly clothed; yet there was sweat upon his brows.
Dully there recurred to him a figure he had employed in one of his talks with Lucy Shannon: that, lacking his faith in her, there would be only emptiness beneath his feet.

===Chinese===
天光在天空里烧尽的时候，一声深沉的叹息从兰亚德唇间逸出；他做了个甘心向凶兆低头的手势，转过身，拖着沉重的脚步横穿城区往回走去。
他半是机器半是活人，沿着第二圈（enceinte，城墙环线）灯火煌煌、喧声刺耳的林荫大道一路跋涉，好几次险些丧命轮下——不是叫疾窜的出租汽车撞上，就是叫笨重轧响的公共马车碾着。
这样的侥幸脱险，他几乎不曾察觉，更谈何在意：得要一记致命的重伤，才压得住他灵魂里那阵无言的、令人发呕的惨痛；这一个钟头里，在他眼里黑下来的，又何止一片落日的天空。
此刻寒气砭骨，他身上的衣裳又不够厚实；可他额头上却沁着汗珠。
他恍恍惚惚想起自己同露西·香农的一次谈话里用过的譬喻：一旦失了对她的信念，他脚下便只剩一片虚空。

===Original===
And now that faith was wanting in him, had been taken from him for all his struggles to retain it; and now indeed he danced on emptiness, the rope of temptation tightening round his neck, the weight of criminal instincts pulling it taut﻿—strangling every right aspiration in him, robbing him of the very breath of that new life to which he had thought to give himself.
If she were not worthy, of what worth the fight?﻿ ﻿…
At one stage of his journey, he turned aside and, more through habit than desire or design, entered a cheap eating-place and consumed his customary evening meal without the slightest comprehension of what he ate or whether the food were good or poor.
When he had finished, he hurried away like a haunted man. There was little room in his mood for sustained thought: his wits were fathoming a bottomless pit of black despair. He felt like a man born blind, through skilful surgery given the boon of sight for a day or two, and suddenly and without any warning thrust back again into darkness.
He knew only that his brief struggle had been all wasted, that behind the flimsy barrier of his honourable ambition, the Lone Wolf was ravening. And he felt that, once he permitted that barrier to be broken down, it could never be repaired.

===Chinese===
而如今，那信念当真从他心里失了踪——任他百般挣扎想留住，终究还是被夺了去；如今他实实在在是在虚空之上跳舞：诱惑的绳索勒上脖颈，越绞越紧，罪性本能的重坠把它绷得笔直——绞死他胸中每一分向上的志气，把那新生的气息也一口夺尽——那新生，他原打算以身相许的。
倘若她当不起这份信念，这一场苦斗又何苦来？
走到半途一处，他拐了进去——多半出于习惯，谈不上什么食欲或盘算——在一家廉价饭铺里把惯常的晚饭吃下肚，至于吃的是什么、滋味好坏，他浑然不知。
一放下刀叉，他便像被鬼撵着似的匆匆走了。他这副心绪，容不下什么绵长的思绪：他那点神明，正在一口深不见底的黑绝望之渊里下坠。他觉得自己像个生来的盲人，靠妙手回春的手术得了一两天重见光明的恩赐，却又毫无预兆，猛地被重新推进黑暗。
他只知道，他那一番短暂的挣扎尽付东流：在那道正派抱负撑起的薄薄屏障后头，独狼正张着血口。而且他明白，这道屏障一经放任崩塌，便再也修不起来了。

===Original===
He had set it up by main strength of will, for love of a woman. He must maintain it now for no incentive other than to retain his own good will﻿—or resign himself utterly to that darkness out of which he had fought his way, to its powers that now beset his soul.
And﻿ ﻿… he didn’t care.
Quite without purpose he sought the machine-shop where he had left his car.
He had no plans; but it was in his mind, a murderous thought, that before another dawn he might encounter Bannon.
Interim, he would go to work. He could think out his problem while driving as readily as in seclusion; whatever he might ultimately elect to do, he could accomplish little before midnight.

===Chinese===
当初他凭一股意志的蛮力把它筑起，是为了对一个女人的爱。如今他必须守住它，可凭的再没有别的指望，只剩留住对自己仅存的一点敬意——否则，便只好把自己整个交还给那片黑暗，交还给此刻围攻他灵魂的黑暗势力——他原是从那里头一路杀出来的。
可是……他不在乎了。
他全然漫无目的地寻到那家机修铺去，他的汽车还寄放在那里。
他没有什么盘算；只是心头压着一个杀气腾腾的念头：赶在下一个天亮之前，他兴许会撞上班农。
眼下的空当，先干活。开着车琢磨他的难题，同闭门独坐一样使得；无论他到头来打算怎么着，午夜之前总归成不了什么事。

===Original===
Toward seven o’clock, with his machine in perfect running order, he took the seat and to the streets in a reckless humour, in the temper of a beast of prey.
The barrier was down: once more the Lone Wolf was on the prowl.
But for the present he controlled himself and acted perfectly his temporary role of taxi-bandit, fellow to those thousands who infest Paris. Half a dozen times in the course of the next three hours people hailed him from sidewalks and restaurants; he took them up, carried them to their several destinations, received payment, and acknowledged their gratuities with perfunctory thanks﻿—thoroughly in character﻿—but all with little conscious thought.
He saw but one thing, the face of Lucy Shannon, white, tense, glimmering wanly in shadow﻿—the countenance with which she had dismissed him.
He had but one thought, the wish to read the riddle of her bondage. To accomplish this he was prepared to go to any extreme; if Bannon and his crew came between him and his purpose, so much the worse for them﻿—and, incidentally, so much the better for society. What might befall himself was of no moment.

===Chinese===
将近七点，机器拾掇得运转如飞，他坐上驾驶座，带着一股不管不顾的心绪驶上街头，一副掠食猛兽的心情。
屏障倒了：独狼再度出巢巡猎。
不过眼下他还按得住自己，把他那临时行当——盘踞巴黎的成千上万个车匪司机中的一员——扮得惟妙惟肖。往后三个钟头里，人们从人行道上、从馆子里先后六次招呼他；他载上他们，送到各自去处，收下车钱，对赏钱也例行公事地道声谢——戏文做得十足——只是这一切，他心不在焉。
他眼里只瞧得见一样东西：露西·香农那张脸，苍白、紧绷，在暗影里泛着幽幽的微光——正是她打发他走时的那副面容。
他心里只转着一个念头：要参透她受制于人的那个哑谜。为着这个，他预备走任何极端；倘若班农和他那一伙横在他和这个目的中间，那他们就更加倒霉——顺带说一句，也就更便宜了社会。至于他自己会落个什么，全不打紧。

===Original===
He entertained but one design, to become again what he had been, the supreme adventurer, the prince of plunderers, to lose himself once more in the delirium of adventurous days and peril-haunted nights, to reincarnate the Lone Wolf and in his guise loot the world anew, to court forgetfulness even at the prison’s gates.﻿ ﻿…
It was after ten when, cruising purposelessly, without a fare, he swung through the rue Auber into the place de l’Opéra and, approaching the Café de la Paix, was hailed by a door-boy of that restaurant.
Drawing in to the curb with the careless address that had distinguished his every action of that evening, he waited, with a throbbing motor, and with mind detached and gaze remote from the streams of foot and wheeled traffic that brawled past on either hand.
After a moment two men issued from the revolving door of the café, and approached the cab. Lanyard paid them no attention. His thoughts were now engaged with a certain hôtel particulier in the neighbourhood of La Muette and, in his preoccupation, he would need only the name of a destination and the sound of the cab-door slammed, to send him off like a shot.
Then he heard one of the men cough heavily, and in a twinkling stiffened to rigidity in his seat. If he had heard that cough but once before, that once had been too often. Without a glance aside, hardening his features to perfect immobility, he knew that the cough was shaking the slighter of those two figures.

===Chinese===
他胸中只怀着一桩图谋：重新做回他本来的人——冒险家中的至尊、掳掠者中的亲王——再度沉入惊险的白昼与凶险缠身的黑夜交逼的迷醉，让独狼借尸还魂，顶着这副皮相把世界重新劫掠一遍，哪怕到了监狱门口，也要把遗忘求到手。……
十点已过，他空车无客、漫无目的地兜着，从奥贝尔街（rue Auber）拐进歌剧院广场（place de l'Opéra），快到和平咖啡馆（Café de la Paix）时，被那家馆子的一个门童招呼住了。
他把车漫不经心地靠向路缘——这一晚他一举一动都是这般洒脱的路数——马达突突震着，等着；心思悬在别处，目光也远离两侧喧喧嚷嚷涌过的人流车流。
不多时，两个男人从咖啡馆的旋转门里出来，朝车子走来。兰亚德没理会他们。他这会儿满脑子是拉米埃特（La Muette）一带的某座私邸（hôtel particulier）；一心旁骛之际，只消报个去处、再听车门砰地一响，就能送他一溜烟蹿出去。
跟着他听见其中一个重重咳嗽起来，霎时间他在驾驶座上僵成了一根铁棍。那咳嗽他哪怕只听过一回，那一回也已听得太多。他眼角都不斜一下，只把面皮绷得纹丝不动，心里明白：那咳嗽正摇着两个人影里身形单薄的那个。

===Original===
And of a sudden he was acutely conscious of the clearness of the frosty atmosphere, of the merciless glare of electricity beating upon him from every side from the numberless street lamps and café lights. And poignantly he regretted neglecting to mask himself with his goggles.
He wasn’t left long in suspense. The coughing died away by spasms; followed the unmistakable, sonorous accents of Bannon.
“Well, my dear boy! I have to thank you for an excellent dinner and a most interesting evening. Pity to break it up so early. Still, les affaires﻿—you know! Sorry you’re not going my way﻿—but that’s a handsome taxi you’ve drawn. What’s its number﻿—eh?”
“Haven’t the faintest notion,” a British voice drawled in response. “Never fret about a taxi’s number until it has run over me.”
“Great mistake,” Bannon rejoined cheerfully. “Always take the number before entering. Then, if anything happens﻿ ﻿… However, that’s a good-looking chap at the wheel﻿—doesn’t look as if he’d run you into any trouble.”

===Chinese===
陡然间，他尖锐地察觉到严寒空气的澄明，察觉到四面八方无数盏路灯和咖啡馆灯火泼下的电光，毫不留情地钉在他身上。他痛悔不已，恨自己疏忽了，没拿风镜把脸罩起来。
他没悬多久的心。一阵阵咳嗽渐渐平息；接踵而至的，是班农那副错认不得的洪钟腔调。
「哎呀，我亲爱的伙计！一顿美餐、一个妙趣横生的夜晚，少不得要谢谢你。可惜散得这样早。不过，les affaires（生意要紧）——你懂的！可惜你不同我一路——不过你今天抽着的这辆出租车倒真精神。几号车来着——呃？」
「半点概念也无，」一个英国嗓子慢吞吞地应道。「出租车还没把我轧倒之前，从来不替它的号码劳神。」
「大错特错，」班农乐呵呵地回敬。「上车之前务必先记车号。这样，万一有个三长两短……话说回来，方向盘前头那小伙子看着不赖——不像会给你招灾惹祸的样子。」

===Original===
“Oh, I fancy not,” said the Englishman, bored.
“Well, you never can tell. The number’s on the lamp. Make a note of it and be on the safe side. Or trust me﻿—I never forget numbers.”
With this speech Bannon ranged alongside Lanyard and looked him over, keenly malicious enjoyment gleaming in his evil old eyes.
“You are an honest-looking chap,” he observed with a mocking smile but in a tone of the most inoffensive admiration﻿—“honest and﻿—ah﻿—what shall I say?﻿—what’s the word we’re all using nowadays?﻿—efficient! Honest and efficient-looking, capable of better things, or I’m no judge! Forgive an old man’s candour, my friend﻿—and take good care of our British cousin here. He doesn’t know his way around Paris very well. Still, I feel confident he’ll come to no harm in *your* company. Here’s a franc for you.”
With matchless effrontery, he produced a coin from the pocket of his fur-lined coat.

===Chinese===
「哦，我也瞧是不会，」那英国人说，意兴阑珊。
「嗯，这可说不准。号码就打在车灯上。记它一笔，图个稳当。再不，信我——我记数目字从来不忘。」
说着，班农踱到兰亚德身旁，把他从头到脚打量一遭，那双老而歹毒的眼睛里闪着刻毒的兴味。
「你是个看着老实的伙计，」他挂着一丝讥诮的笑品评道，语调里却是最无害的钦慕——「老实，而且——啊——叫我说什么好呢？——如今人人挂在嘴边的那个词儿是什么来着？——高效率！一副老实又高效的长相，能担更大的事，不然我就白长这双识人的眼！原谅老头子口直，我的朋友——把咱们这位英国表亲照看好喽。他不大认得巴黎的路。不过我放心得很，他在*你*手上出不了乱子。这个法郎赏你。」
他脸皮厚得登峰造极，从毛皮里子大衣的口袋里摸出一枚硬币。

===Original===
Unhesitatingly, permitting no expression to colour his features, Lanyard extended his palm, received the money, dropped it into his own pocket, and carried two fingers to the visor of his cap.
“Merci, monsieur,” he said evenly.
“Ah, that’s the right spirit!” the deep voice jeered. “Never be above your station, my man﻿—never hesitate to take a tip! Here, I’ll give you another, gratis: get out of this business: you’re too good for it. Don’t ask me how I know; I can tell by your face﻿—Hello! Why do you turn down the flag? You haven’t started yet!”
“Conversation goes up on the clock,” Lanyard replied stolidly in French. He turned and faced Bannon squarely, loosing a glance of venomous hatred into the other’s eyes. “The longer I have to stop here listening to your senile monologue, the more you’ll have to pay. What address, please?” he added, turning back to get a glimpse of his passenger.
“Hôtel Astoria,” the porter supplied.

===Chinese===
兰亚德毫不含糊，容不得半点神色上脸，摊开手掌接了钱，丢进自己兜里，两根手指往帽檐上一碰。
「Merci, monsieur（谢谢您，先生），」他四平八稳地说。
「啊，这就对了，这态度才像话！」那深沉嗓音讥嘲道，「千万别嫌行当低贱，伙计——赏钱嘛，只管伸手！得，再白送你一句，不收钱：趁早改行：你这身本事干这个屈才。别问我怎么知道的；看你这张脸就瞧得出来——咦，喂！你翻计程旗做什么？车还没上道呢！」
「闲聊的工夫，『钟表』上可照样跳字，」兰亚德用法语木然答道。他转过脸，正对着班农，朝那双眼睛放出一瞥淬毒的恨。「我在这儿多听一刻你这套老糊涂的独白，你就得多掏一刻的钱。请问，去哪个地址？」说着他又转回身去，好瞥一眼他的乘客。
「阿斯托里亚旅馆（Hôtel Astoria），」门童报了去处。

===Original===
“Very good.”
The porter closed the door.
“But remember my advice,” Bannon counselled coolly, stepping back and waving his hand to the man in the cab. “Good night.”
Lanyard took his car smartly away from the curb, wheeled round the corner into the boulevard des Capucines, and toward the rue Royale.
He had gone but a block when the window at his back was lowered and his fare observed pleasantly:

===Chinese===
「好嘞。」
门童关上了车门。
「不过记住我的忠告，」班农冷冷地叮嘱，退后一步，朝车里那位挥了挥手。「晚安。」
兰亚德干净利落地把车驶离路缘，拐过街角上了卡皮西纳大道（boulevard des Capucines），往罗亚尔街（rue Royale）方向去。
才开过一个街区，身后的车窗便摇了下来，乘客语声和悦地开了腔：

===Original===
“That you, Lanyard?”
The adventurer hesitated an instant; then, without looking round, responded:
“Wertheimer, eh?”
“Right-O! The old man had me puzzled for a minute with his silly chaffing. Stupid of me, too, because we’d just been talking about you.”
“Had you, though!”

===Chinese===
「是你吗，兰亚德？」
冒险家迟疑了一瞬；随即并不回头，应道：
「维特海默，嗯？」
「正是！老头子那通耍宝，懵了我好一阵子。也怪我自己蠢，咱们方才还正说起你来着。」
「说起我来着，可不是嘛！」

===Original===
“Rather. Hadn’t you better take me where we can have a quiet little talk?”
“I’m not conscious of the necessity﻿—”
“Oh, I say!” Wertheimer protested amiably﻿—“don’t be shirty, old top. Give a chap a chance. Besides, I have a bit of news from Antwerp that I guarantee will interest you.”
“Antwerp?” Lanyard iterated, mystified.
“Antwerp, where the ships sail from,” Wertheimer laughed: “not Amsterdam, where the diamonds flock together, as you may know.”

===Chinese===
「可不是。你何不拉我去个能安安静静说几句的地方？」
「我不觉得有什么必要——」
「哎，话不能这么说！」维特海默好脾气地抗议——「别动气嘛，老兄。总得给人个机会。再说，我带了条安特卫普（Antwerp）来的消息，包管你爱听。」
「安特卫普？」兰亚德一头雾水地重了一遍。
「安特卫普，轮船起锚的地方，」维特海默笑道——「不是钻石扎堆的阿姆斯特丹（Amsterdam），这你该是知道的。」

===Original===
“I don’t follow you, I’m afraid.”
“I shan’t elucidate until we’re under cover.”
“All right. Where shall I take you?”
“Any quiet café will do. You must know one﻿—”
“Thanks﻿—no,” said Lanyard dryly. “If I must confabulate with gentlemen of your kidney, I prefer to keep it dark. Even dressed as I am, I might be recognized, you know.”

===Chinese===
「恕我跟不上你的话头。」
「不到遮人耳目的地方，我无可奉告。」
「行吧。送你去哪儿？」
「随便哪家清静咖啡馆都成。你总归认得一家——」
「多谢——不必，」兰亚德干巴巴地说。「倘若非得同你这路人密谈，我宁可拣个不见天日的去处。就算是我如今这身行头，你也知道，保不齐还叫人认出来。」

===Original===
But it was evident that Wertheimer didn’t mean to permit himself to be ruffled.
“Then will my modest diggings do?” he suggested pleasantly. “I’ve taken a suite in the rue Vernet, just back of the Hôtel Astoria, where we can be as private as you please, if you’ve no objection.”
“None whatever.”
Wertheimer gave him the number and replaced the window.﻿ ﻿…
His rooms in the rue Vernet proved to be a small ground-floor apartment with private entrance to the street.

===Chinese===
不过，维特海默显然不打算为这点言语着恼。
「那么，我那寒舍将就将就？」他愉快地提议。「我在维尔内街（rue Vernet）租了一套屋子，就在阿斯托里亚旅馆背后，要多清静有多清静——只要你不嫌弃。」
「不嫌弃。」
维特海默报了门牌号，摇上了车窗。……
他在维尔内街的这处居所，原来是底层（rez-de-chaussée）一套小小的公寓，独门独户，直通街面。

===Original===
“Took the tip from you,” he told Lanyard as he unlocked the door. “I daresay you’d be glad to get back to that *rez-de-chaussée* of yours. Ripping place, that.﻿ ﻿… By the way﻿—judging from your apparently robust state of health, you haven’t been trying to live at home of late.”
“Indeed?”
“Indeed yes, monsieur! If I may presume to advise﻿—I’d pull wide of the rue Roget for a while﻿—for as long, at least, as you remain in your present intractable temper.”
“Daresay you’re right,” Lanyard assented carelessly, following, as Wertheimer turned up the lights, into a modest salon cosily furnished. “You live here alone, I understand?”
“Quite: make yourself perfectly at ease; nobody can hear us. And,” the Englishman added with a laugh, “do forget your pistol, Mr. Lanyard. I’m not Popinot, nor is this Troyon’s.”

===Chinese===
「这一招还是跟你学的，」他开着锁对兰亚德说。「我敢说，你自己那处 *rez-de-chaussée*（底层），你巴不得回去呢。妙极了，那地方。……对了——瞧你这副红光满面的康健模样，近来是不大敢回家住吧。」
「是吗？」
「可不是嘛，先生！恕我斗胆进一言——罗热街（rue Roget）那一带，你还是绕着走些日子吧——至少，趁你如今这副不服帖的脾气还没改。」
「怕是叫你说着了。」兰亚德漫不经心地应着，随开了灯的维特海默走进一间陈设温馨的朴素客厅。「据我所知，你独自住在这儿？」
「正是：你只管放宽心；没人听得见咱们说话。还有，」英国人笑着添了一句，「兰亚德先生，把你的手枪忘了吧。我不是波皮诺，这儿也不是特鲁瓦旅馆。」

===Original===
“Still,” Lanyard countered, “you’ve just been dining with Bannon.”
Wertheimer laughed easily. “Had me there!” he admitted, unabashed. “I take it you know a bit more about the Old Man than you did a week ago?”
“Perhaps.”
“But sit down: take that chair there, which commands both doors, if you don’t trust me.”
“Do you think I ought to?”

===Chinese===
「话虽如此，」兰亚德回敬，「你方才还同班农共进晚餐来着。」
维特海默坦然一笑。「这一下可给你逮着了！」他毫不脸红地认了。「我猜，比起一个礼拜前，你对那位老头子的底细，多少多知道些了？」
「也许。」
「不过请坐：你要是信不过我，就坐那边那把椅子，两扇门都罩得住。」
「你觉得我该信不过吗？」

===Original===
“Hardly. Otherwise I’d ask you to take my word that you’re safe for the time being. As it is, I shan’t be offended if you keep your gun handy and your sense of self-preservation running under forced draught. But you won’t refuse to join me in a whiskey and soda?”
“No,” said Lanyard slowly﻿—“not if you drink from the same bottle.”
Again the Englishman laughed unaffectedly as he fetched a decanter, glasses, bottled soda, and a box of cigarettes, and placed them within Lanyard’s reach.
The adventurer eyed him narrowly, puzzled. He knew nothing of this man, beyond his reputation﻿—something unsavoury enough, in all conscience!﻿—had seen him only once, and then from a distance, before that conference in the rue Chaptal. And now he was becoming sensitive to a personality uncommonly insinuating: Wertheimer was displaying all the poise of an Englishman of the better caste. More than anybody in the underworld that Lanyard had ever known this blackmailer had an air of one acquainted with his own respect. And his nonchalance, the good nature with which he accepted Lanyard’s pardonable distrust, his genial assumption of fellowship and a common footing, attracted even as it intrigued.
With the easy courtesy of a practised host, he measured whiskey into Lanyard’s glass till checked by a “Thank you,” then helped himself generously, and opened the soda.

===Chinese===
「大可不必。不然，我就要请你凭我一句话，担保你眼下安然无恙了。既然如此，你手枪只管备在手边，保命的那根弦只管绷到十二分，我决不见怪。可陪我喝杯威士忌苏打，你总不至于拒绝吧？」
「不至于，」兰亚德缓缓道——「只要你我同瓶共斟。」
英国人又由衷地笑了，取来一瓶威士忌、几只杯子、瓶装苏打水和一盒香烟，都放到兰亚德够得着的地方。
冒险家眯眼细看他，满心纳罕。此人他实在一无所知，只闻其名——凭良心讲，那名声也够难听的了！——沙普塔尔街（rue Chaptal）那场会谈之前统共见过一面，还是远远的。而此刻他渐渐嗅出此人身上有一股异样绵密的气场：维特海默拿出来的，是上等门第英国人的从容不迫。兰亚德平生在黑道里所识的人，数这个敲诈犯最有一副自重自敬的气度。而他那份闲逸，他领受兰亚德这番情有可原的猜疑时的好脾气，他俨然平辈论交的亲热假设——处处撩人好奇，又处处讨人欢喜。
他以练达主人从容的礼数往兰亚德杯里斟威士忌，直到一声「谢谢」才收手，随后给自己满满斟上，开了苏打水。

===Original===
“I’ll not ask you to drink with me,” he said with a twinkle, “but﻿—chin-chin!”﻿—and tilting his glass, half-emptied it at a draught.
Muttering formally, at a disadvantage and resenting it, Lanyard drank with less enthusiasm if without misgivings.
Wertheimer selected a cigarette and lighted it at leisure.
“Well,” he laughed through a cloud of smoke﻿—“I think we’re fairly on our way to an understanding, considering you told me to go to hell when last we met!”
His spirit was irresistible: in spite of himself Lanyard returned the smile. “I never knew a man to take it with better grace,” he admitted, lighting his own cigarette.

===Chinese===
「我不邀你同我共饮，」他眼波一闪，说道，「不过——chin-chin（干杯）！」——说罢倾起杯子，一口气去了半盏。
兰亚德拘礼地咕哝了一句，落了下风又心有不甘，把酒喝了——兴头欠着，疑虑倒无。
维特海默拣起一支烟，从从容容点上。
「喏，」他隔着一团烟雾笑道——「想想咱们上回见面，你还让我下地狱去；照这么算，咱们眼下算是顺顺当当走上互相谅解的道儿了！」
他这股劲头叫人推拒不掉：兰亚德不由自主也报以一笑。「挨了这一句还能这般雍容领受的，我生平头一回见，」他承认道，点起自己的烟。

===Original===
“Why not! I *liked* it: you gave us precisely what we asked for.”
“Then,” Lanyard demanded gravely, “if that’s your viewpoint, if you’re decent enough to see it that way﻿—what the devil are you doing in that galley?”
“Mischief makes strange bedfellows, you’ll admit. And if you think that a fair question﻿—what are you doing here, with me?”
“Same excuse as before﻿—trying to find out what your game is.”
Wertheimer eyed the ceiling with an intimate grin. “My dear fellow!” he protested﻿—“all *you* want to know is everything!”

===Chinese===
「为什么不！我*喜欢*着呢：你给我们的，恰恰就是我们开口讨的那句话。」
「既然如此，」兰亚德正色发问，「倘若这就是你的立场，倘若你为人还算体面、肯这样想——你到底上了那条贼船做什么？」
「落难不择伴，同床无奇不有，这你总得承认。再说，你要觉得这问题问得公道——那你这会儿在我这儿，又算做什么？」
「理由照旧——想弄清你们玩的哪一出。」
维特海默咧嘴一笑，眼睛亲亲热热地望着天花板。「我亲爱的伙计！」他抗议道——「*你*想知道的，是——一切的一切！」

===Original===
“More or less,” Lanyard admitted gracelessly. “One gathers that you mean to stop this side the Channel for some time.”
“How so?”
“There’s a settled, personal atmosphere about this establishment. It doesn’t look as if half your things were still in trunks.”
“Oh, these digs! Yes, they are comfy.”
“You don’t miss London?”

===Chinese===
「大差不差，」兰亚德不客气地承认。「瞧这光景，你打算在海峡这一边盘桓些时日了。」
「从何说起？」
「这处宅子透着一股安家落户的人味儿。瞧着不像你还有半数家什仍原封锁在箱子里。」
「哦，说这窝！可不是，住着挺受用。」
「不想伦敦么？」

===Original===
“Rather! But I shall appreciate it all the more when I go back.”
“Then you can go back, if you like?”
“Meaning your impression is, I made it too hot for me?” Wertheimer interposed with a quizzical glance. “I shan’t tell you about that. But I’m hoping to be able to run home for an occasional weekend without vexing Scotland Yard. Why not come with me some time?”
Lanyard shook his head.
“Come!” the Englishman rallied him. “Don’t put on so much side. I’m not bad company. Why not be sociable, since we’re bound to be thrown together more or less in the way of business.”

===Chinese===
「想得慌！不过等回去那日，只会更加受用。」
「这么说，你想回去，随时回得去？」
「你是觉得，是我把自己烘得太烫、待不住了？」维特海默斜插一句，眼色里带着揶揄。「这一节我无可奉告。不过我指望着，往后偶尔也能跑回家过个周末，不劳苏格兰场（Scotland Yard）操心。哪天随我一道去如何？」
兰亚德摇了摇头。
「嗳！」英国人撺掇他。「别搭这么大的架子。我这个人做伴并不赖。反正生意场上迟早抬头不见低头见，何不彼此亲和些？」

===Original===
“Oh, I think not.”
“But, my dear chap, you can’t keep this up. Playing taxiway man is hardly your shop. And of course you understand you won’t be permitted to engage in any more profitable pursuit until you make terms with the powers that be﻿—or leave Paris.”
“Terms with Bannon, De Morbihan, Popinot and yourself﻿—eh?”
“With the same.”
“Mr. Wertheimer,” Lanyard told him quietly, “none of you will stop me if ever I make up my mind to take the field again.”

===Chinese===
「哦，我看不必。」
「可是，我亲爱的伙计，这一套你撑不长的。开出租车断然不是你的本行。再说你自然明白，不同当道诸位讲好条款——或者干脆离开巴黎，更来钱的营生，人家是一桩也不许你沾的。」
「同班农、德·莫尔比昂、波皮诺，还有阁下你——讲条款，呃？」
「正是同这几位。」
「维特海默先生，」兰亚德平平静静地对他说，「哪天我当真拿定主意重开炉灶，你们一个也拦我不住。」

===Original===
“You haven’t been thinking of quitting it﻿—what?” Wertheimer demanded innocently, opening his eyes wide.
“Perhaps﻿ ﻿…”
“Ah, now I begin to see a light! So that’s the reason you’ve come down to tooling a taxi. I wondered! But somehow, Mr. Lanyard”﻿—Wertheimer’s eyes narrowed thoughtfully﻿—“I can hardly see you content with that line﻿ ﻿… even if this reform notion isn’t simple swank!”
“Well, what do you think?”
“I think,” the Englishman laughed﻿—“*I* think this conference doesn’t get anywhere in particular. Our simple, trusting natures don’t seem to fraternize as spontaneously as they might. We may as well cut the sparring and go, down to business﻿—don’t you think? But before we do, I’d like your leave to offer one word of friendly advice.”

===Chinese===
「你该不是一直在盘算着金盆洗手吧——啊？」维特海默一脸无辜地追问，眼睛瞪得溜圆。
「也许……」
「啊，这下我算见着亮儿了！原来如此，你才屈尊降贵开起了出租车。我先前还纳闷来着！不过不知怎么，兰亚德先生，」——维特海默若有所思地眯起眼——「我横竖看不出你安于这一路……哪怕你这改邪归正的念头并不光是摆个姿态！」
「那么，依你怎么看？」
「依我看啊，」英国人笑道——「*我*是看，咱们这场会谈谈不出个所以然。咱们两家天真轻信的性情，瞧着没能像本该的那样一拍即合。与其继续这么试探着过招，索性开门见山谈正事——你说呢？不过开谈之前，我想讨你个恩准，奉送一句友好的忠告。」

===Original===
“And that is﻿—?”
“ ’Ware Bannon!”
Lanyard nodded. “Thanks,” he said simply.
“I say that in all sincerity,” Wertheimer declared. “God knows you’re nothing to me, but at least you’ve played the game like a man; and I won’t see you butchered to make an Apache holiday for want of warning.”
“Bannon’s as vindictive as that, you think?”

===Chinese===
「忠告是——？」
「当心班农！」
兰亚德点了点头。「多谢，」他简单地说。
「这话字字出于至诚，」维特海默声明。「老天知道，你于我算不得什么；可至少你行事像条汉子，讲究规矩；我不忍看你光因为没人递句话，就白白挨宰，给阿帕奇们做一晚余兴。」
「依你看，班农狠毒到这个地步？」

===Original===
“Holds you in the most poisonous regard, if you ask me. Perhaps you know why: I don’t. Anyway, it was rotten luck that brought your car to the door tonight. He named you during dinner, and while apparently he doesn’t know where to look for you, it is plain he’s got no use for you﻿—not, at least, until your attitude towards the organization changes.”
“It hasn’t. But I’m obliged.”
“Sure you can’t see your way to work with us?”
“Absolutely.”
“Mind you, I’ll have to report to the Old Man. I’ve got to tell him your answer.”

===Chinese===
「要我说，他对你的怨毒算是毒到了家。缘故你也许知道：我是不知道。反正，今晚把你的车引到这门口，是走了霉运。饭桌上他点了你的名，虽说看他那样子并不知道上哪儿寻你，可明摆着他容不下你——至少，要等你对组织的态度变一变。」
「态度没变。不过领你这份情。」
「当真寻不出个门道，同我们搭伙？」
「当真。」
「你可记着，我得向老头子回报。你的回话，我总得捎给他。」

===Original===
“I don’t think I need tell you what to tell him,” said Lanyard with a grin.
“Still, it’s worth thinking over. I know the Old Man’s mind well enough to feel safe in offering you any inducement you can name, in reason, if you’ll come to us. Ten thousand francs in your pocket before morning, if you like, and freedom to chuck this filthy job of yours﻿—”
“Please stop there!” Lanyard interrupted hotly. “I was beginning to like you, too﻿ ﻿… Why persist in reminding me you’re intimate with the brute who had Roddy butchered in his sleep?”
“Poor devil!” Wertheimer said gently. “That was a sickening business, I admit. But who told you﻿—?”
“Never mind. It’s true, isn’t it?”

===Chinese===
「该怎么回他，我看不必我教吧，」兰亚德咧嘴一笑。
「话虽如此，到底值得掂量掂量。老头子的心思我摸得透，尽可放胆讲：只要入伙，凡是你开得出的价——不出大格的——我无不应承。天亮前叫你兜里揣上一万法郎，随你挑，还你自由身，把这身腌臜营生一甩了之——」
「请到此为止！」兰亚德勃然打断。「我都快喜欢你了……何必偏要提醒我，你同那头睡梦里叫人宰了罗迪的畜生交情莫逆？」
「可怜的家伙！」维特海默轻声说。「那桩事确实腌臜，我认。可这是谁告诉你的——？」
「不劳你问。是真的，对不对？」

===Original===
“Yes,” the Englishman admitted gravely﻿—“it’s true. It lies at Bannon’s door, when all’s said.﻿ ﻿… Perhaps you won’t believe me, but it’s a fact I didn’t know positively who was responsible till tonight.”
“You don’t really expect me to swallow that? You were hand-in-glove﻿—”
“Ah, but on probation only! When they voted Roddy out, I wasn’t consulted. They kept me in the dark﻿—mostly, I flatter myself, because I draw the line at murder. If I had known﻿—this you won’t believe, of course﻿—Roddy would be alive today.”
“I’d like to believe you,” Lanyard admitted. “But when you ask me to sign articles with that damned assassin﻿—!”
“You can’t play our game with clean hands,” Wertheimer retorted.

===Chinese===
「对，」英国人沉沉地认了——「是真的。千说万说，这笔账记在班农名下。……你多半不肯信：直到今夜，我才知道那件事确凿是谁的手笔。」
「你总不会当真指望我囫囵咽下这话吧？你们本就穿一条连裆裤——」
「啊，可我只算个试用期的伙计！他们投票除名罗迪的时候，没人问我一句。他们把我蒙在鼓里——我孤芳自赏地想，多半是因为杀人这条线，我不肯过。倘若我当时知道——这句你自然也不信——罗迪今儿还活着。」
「我倒乐意信你，」兰亚德承认。「可你却要我同那个天杀的刺客画押入伙——！」
「干我们这行当的，手就别想干净，」维特海默反唇相讥。

===Original===
Lanyard found no answer to that.
“If you’ve said all you wished to,” he suggested, rising, “I can assure you my answer is final﻿—and go about my business.”
“What’s your hurry? Sit down. There’s more to say﻿—much more.”
“As for instance﻿—?”
“I had a fancy you might like to put a question or two.”

===Chinese===
这一句，兰亚德无话可答。
「你要说的若都说了，」他起身道，「那我可以向你担保，我的答复是最终版——就此各奔前程吧。」
「急什么？坐下。话还多着呢——多得很。」
「比如——？」
「我有这样一个感觉，你兴许乐意问上一两句。」

===Original===
Lanyard shook his head; it was plain that Wertheimer designed to draw him out through his interest in Lucy Shannon.
“I haven’t the slightest curiosity concerning your affairs,” he observed.
“But you should have; I could tell you a great many interesting things that intimately affect *your* affairs, if I liked. You must understand that I shall hold the balance of power here, from now on.”
“Congratulations!” Lanyard laughed derisively.
“No joke, my dear chap: I’ve been promoted over the heads of your friends, De Morbihan and Popinot, and shall henceforth be﻿—as they say in America﻿—the whole works.”

===Chinese===
兰亚德摇头；明摆着，维特海默打的算盘，是想借露西·香农勾他的魂，把他的话套出来。
「你们的事，我半点不好奇，」他说。
「可你本该好奇才是；只要我乐意，多多少少桩桩跟*你的*事切身相关的趣闻，我都讲得出。你务必明白：从今往后，这边执掌权衡的，是我。」
「恭喜恭喜！」兰亚德嗤笑出声。
「不是玩笑，我亲爱的伙计：我已经连跳几级，越过了你那几位朋友德·莫尔比昂和波皮诺的头顶，从今往后便是——照美国人的说法——独揽全盘的总当家。」

===Original===
“By what warrant?”
“The illustrious Bannon’s. I’ve been appointed his lieutenant﻿—vice Greggs, deposed for bungling.”
“Do you mean to tell me Bannon controls De Morbihan and Popinot?”
The Englishman smiled indulgently. “If you didn’t know it, he’s commander-in-chief of our allied forces, presiding genius of the International Underworld Unlimited.”
“Bosh!” cried Lanyard contemptuously. “Why talk to me as if I were a child, to be frightened by a bogey-tale like that?”

===Chinese===
「奉谁的号令？」
「凭那位声名赫赫的班农。我受任他的副手——顶替办事砸了锅、丢了官的格雷格斯。」
「你是要告诉我，德·莫尔比昂和波皮诺都归班农调遣？」
英国人宽容地一笑。「你要是不知道，我说与你听：他是我们联军统帅的总司令，『国际黑社会无限公司』（International Underworld Unlimited）的掌舵天才。」
「胡说八道！」兰亚德满眼不屑地嚷道。「拿这种妖魔鬼怪的故事，把我当三岁小孩吓唬，算什么意思？」

===Original===
“Take it or leave it: the fact remains.﻿ ﻿… I know, if you don’t. I confess I didn’t till tonight; but I’ve learned some things that have opened my eyes.﻿ ﻿… You see, we had a table in a quiet corner of the Café de la Paix, and since the Old Man’s sailing for home before long it was time for him to unbosom rather thoroughly to the man he leaves to represent him in London and Paris. I never suspected our power before he began to talk.﻿ ﻿…”
Lanyard, watching the man closely, would have sworn he had never seen one more sober. He was indescribably perplexed by this ostensible candour﻿—mystified and mistrustful.
“And then there’s this to be considered, from your side,” Wertheimer resumed with the most businesslike manner: “you can work with us without being obliged to deal in any way with the Old Man or De Morbihan, or Popinot. Bannon will never cross the Atlantic again, and you can do pretty much as you like, within reason﻿—subject to my approval, that is.”
“One of us is mad,” Lanyard commented profoundly.
“One of us is blind to his best interests,” Wertheimer amended with entire good-humour.

===Chinese===
「信不信由你：事实终归是事实。……我是知道了，你不知道罢了。我承认，直到今夜我也一样不知道；可我长了些见识，开了眼。……是这么回事：我们在和平咖啡馆一个清静角落占了一张桌子，老头子不久便要扬帆回老家，也该到时候向他留在伦敦与巴黎的全权代表，掏一掏彻底的心窝子了。他不开口则已，一开口，我竟从不曾疑心过我们的势力有这般大。……」
兰亚德紧盯着此人，敢赌咒从没见过谁比他更不像说笑。这副表面上的坦荡弄得他困惑莫名——又是疑云满腹，又是不能全然不信。
「再者，还有一层，替你这头考虑，」维特海默拿出十足办公事的口吻接着说：「你尽可同我们共事，全不必同老头子、德·莫尔比昂或波皮诺打半点交道。班农从此不会再横渡大西洋，你呢，只要不出大格，尽可为所欲为——当然喽，须得我点头。」
「你我两个，有一个疯了，」兰亚德高深莫测地评了一句。
「你我两个，有一个瞧不出什么才最合自己的利益，」维特海默一团和气地修正道。

===Original===
“Perhaps﻿ ﻿… Let it go at that. I’m not interested﻿—never did care for fairy tales.”
“Don’t go yet. There is still much to be said on both sides of the argument.”
“Has there been one?”
“Besides, I promised you news from Antwerp.”
“To be sure,” Lanyard said, and paused, his curiosity at length engaged.

===Chinese===
「也许……就此打住吧。我没有兴致——童话，我打小就不爱听。」
「先别走。这场辩论，两边都还有的是话没说。」
「方才那也算一场辩论？」
「再说，安特卫普的消息，我可是答允过你的。」
「说的是，」兰亚德应了一声，顿了一顿——他的好奇心，总算给勾起来了。

===Original===
Wertheimer delved into the breast-pocket of his dress-coat and produced a blue telegraph-form, handing it to the adventurer.
Of even date, from Antwerp, it read:

> Underworld﻿—Paris﻿—Greggs arrested today boarding steamer for America after desperate struggle killed himself immediately afterward poison no confession﻿—Q-2.

===Chinese===
维特海默往礼服胸袋里一探，抽出一张蓝色电报纸，递到冒险家手里。
电文发自安特卫普，即日拍来，写道：

> 地下世界（Underworld）——巴黎——格雷格斯今日登轮赴美被捕殊死挣扎后旋即服毒自尽未吐口供——Q-2。

===Original===
“*Underworld?*” Lanyard queried blankly.
“Our telegraphic address, of course. ‘Q-2’ is our chief factor in Antwerp.”
“So they got Greggs!”
“Stupid oaf,” Wertheimer observed; “I’ve no sympathy for him. The whole affair was a blunder, from first to last.”
“But you got Greggs out and burned Troyon’s﻿—!”

===Chinese===
「*地下世界*？」兰亚德一脸茫然地问道。
「我们的电报挂号，自然。『Q-2』是我们坐镇安特卫普的总代理。」
「这么说，格雷格斯终究叫他们拿了！」
「蠢牛一头，」维特海默评道；「我对他没有半分同情。这桩事从头至尾，就是一场昏着儿。」
「可你们把格雷格斯捞了出去，还烧了特鲁瓦旅馆——！」

===Original===
“Still our friends at the Préfecture weren’t satisfied. Something must have roused their suspicions.”
“You don’t know what?”
“There must have been a leak somewhere﻿—”
“If so, it would certainly have led the police to me, after all the pains you were at to saddle me with the crime. There’s something more than simple treachery in this, Mr. Wertheimer.”
“Perhaps you’re right,” said the other thoughtfully.

===Chinese===
「可警察总局那班朋友到底意难平。总有什么东西，勾起了他们的疑心。」
「不知道是什么？」
「准是哪一处走了风——」
